**THE PRE-LANDING CHECKS GAIN A THIRD QUESTION, EARNED BY MEASUREMENT ON 2026-08-05.** ADR-0074's §6 declared *"31 call sites unwrap."* The type, all eleven signatures and the barrel export were then landed so the compiler could enumerate the surface, and the real figure is **~40+ sites across three packages** — after which the change was reverted to a clean tree, **the measurement being the thing worth keeping.** **The gap was not an undercount; it was a category the count could not contain: §6 COUNTED CONSUMERS AND OMITTED IMPLEMENTORS.** Every inline adapter object in a conformance test *implements* the SPI and breaks the moment a signature moves — and **a call-site search is structurally blind to them**, because an implementor never names the method it satisfies in a form that search matches. **It generalises and it is not about reads: ADR-0072 and ADR-0073 both landed without this being stated**, and were fortunate rather than correct in having their consumers and implementors edited together. The three checks now read: *(1) does this value match what its producer is positioned to know — in either direction?* · *(2) if this mechanism ships, what flows through it on the first real run?* · **(3) when an interface changes, who IMPLEMENTS it — including every fixture — and not only who CALLS it?** **The third is the cheapest of the three to answer and was the one nobody asked**, twice before, on the two ADRs this one extends.

**THE PRE-LANDING CHECKS GAIN A FOURTH QUESTION, EARNED BY MEASUREMENT ON 2026-08-05 AND CARRYING A STANDING COUNT (D-087).** ADR-0077 §6 step 4 scoped a component's re-founding to **ten fields of `ReasoningHarvest.state`**. The registry also consumes `harvest.audit` — **as proof the reasoning happened** — and refuses any capability publishing without it. Measured on the canonical path: **0 of 18 declared evidence names matched, and no package body was produced at all.** The fourth check reads: **(4) what does this step's subject CONSUME, as opposed to what does it PRODUCE?**

**IT IS THE THIRD INSTANCE OF ONE SHAPE IN THREE SECTIONS, AND THE COUNT IS PART OF THE CHECK.** ADR-0061 §6 scoped a file by its exports and was wrong about the gateway's routing (D-076); ADR-0077 §4.8 scoped a gate by its properties and was wrong about step 1's own ordering (D-081); ADR-0077 §6 step 4 scoped a harvest by its fields and was wrong about the registry's evidence channel. **All three scoped by PRODUCED values; all three were wrong about a CONSUMED one.** The measurement that would have caught the third is one grep for what `reasoning-publication.ts` reads off `harvest`, and it takes a minute — which puts this alongside check (3) as cheap and unasked.

**THE STANDING COUNT, KEPT DELIBERATELY: a fourth occurrence in ADR-0077 makes this a property of the DOCUMENT rather than of three steps, and that is a different finding with a different repair.** Recorded as a count rather than a closed item because no gate can read an ADR's step scope against the consumption graph of the module it names — D-011's limit, and the reason all four of these are questions a person asks rather than checks a suite runs.

> **THE COUNT REACHED FOUR THE SAME DAY, AND WHAT IT EARNED IS A METHOD RULE ATTACHED TO CHECK (4) RATHER THAN A FIFTH INSTANCE (D-090).** Executing §6 steps 4 and 5 surfaced two further consumers §4.3 did not see — the emitter-derived automation state four registry publishers read, and the observed-outcome channel `reflectViaFTE` needs.
>
> **THE METHOD RULE, WHICH IS WHAT CHECK (4) IS ACTUALLY ASKING FOR:** ***reading a producer's code to enumerate its consumers finds only the consumers the producer already knows about.*** ADR-0077 §4.3 enumerated the re-composition by walking **the bridge's own call sequence** — translate, run, ground, publish, assemble — which is the method the check forbids, applied by the document that introduced the check's subject. Every gap since has been a consumer of that sequence the walk was structurally unable to see.
>
> **The counter-method is not more diligence, it is a different direction of search: enumerate from the CONSUMERS.** For `ReasoningHarvest` that is one grep over what `reasoning-publication.ts` reads off `harvest`, and it would have surfaced `state`, `audit`, `state.automation` and `state.automationManifest` **at once** — all four gaps, in one pass, before any of them cost a measurement cycle. **Four sequential discoveries and one grep are the same information; only the order differs, and the order is the whole cost.**

> **THE RULE'S FIRST PRE-WORK CATCH, 2026-08-05 — AND EVERY PRIOR INSTANCE WAS CAUGHT AFTER THE WORK.**
>
> ADR-0077 §6 step 8 needed a new field on `ExecutionResult`. **I priced it to the programme owner as reaching *"every conformance fixture that constructs one"*** — the call-site instinct, and the same estimate shape D-087 counts. Applying the rule instead and enumerating **from the constructors**: **`ExecutionResult` has ONE constructor**, `domains/execution.ts`. The four conformance tests that name the type obtain one by **running the domain**, never by literal, and it is **not** in `verify-contract-compatibility`'s frozen fixture corpus.
>
> **Measured price: one constructor, zero fixtures, no frozen contract. Call-site enumeration would have suggested seventeen files.**
>
> **This is the first time in the register the rule was applied BEFORE the work rather than derived from its cost afterwards.** D-076, D-081, §6 step 4 and D-090's two causes were all found by building into them. The distinction matters because the rule is cheap only in this direction: **enumerating constructors is one grep and it is available at estimate time**, which is the only moment an estimate can be corrected.

**THE G-5 FIX WAS OBSERVED CATCHING ITS OWN DEFECT CLASS IN THE WILD, ONE COMMIT AFTER LANDING.** `a81c079` made `AgentCatalogue.invoke` lead with the fact and follow with the declaration. **Twice during the batch-3 reading — the very next piece of work — an incomplete probe fixture faulted an agent, and both times the message read correctly:** `agent authoring.quality-review: TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'length') — declared handling: Every check reports its own verdict, so a refusal names the rule that failed rather than the case as a whole.` **Before the fix that would have opened with *"Every check reports its own verdict…"*** — a confident statement about refusal semantics, for a fault that was a missing property on a fixture. **A defect class caught in the wild by its own fix within a single commit is stronger evidence that the fix was correctly aimed than the test that proves it**, because the test was written by the same person who chose the fix, and the wild instance was not chosen at all. Recorded for that reason, not as a success note.

**THE G-5 TEST'S FIRST ASSERTION WAS WRONG, AND THE ASSERTION WAS CORRECTED RATHER THAN THE CODE.** It required the message to *start* with `TypeError:`, and it failed: `AgentError` legitimately prefixes the agent id, so the message begins `agent x.faulty: TypeError: …`. **The prefix is correct and belongs there** — an operator needs to know WHICH agent faulted before knowing how — so the assertion was retargeted at the first *explanation* offered rather than the first character. **The tempting move was the other one**: the code had just been written to satisfy this test, the test had just been written to prove the code, and dropping the prefix would have made both green. **That is D-041's pressure in miniature** — adjusting the thing being measured to fit the measurement — and it is the same shape as refusing to admit a compound prefix so the nine `sync.design-*` agents could pass AN-1. **Recorded because the pressure appears at a scale small enough to act on without noticing**, which is where it is most dangerous: no ruling was sought for a one-line assertion, and none would have been.

**R-13.7 WAS SATISFIED BEFORE IT COULD BE RECORDED, and the distinction is worth keeping.** The rule has two halves: a *demonstration* — the property is shown to fail, by its own branch — and a *machine-readable record* of that demonstration in the governance registry. For `verify-agent-naming` the demonstration was complete and reproducible while the registry still could not hold it: three probes ran, two proved their branch, the third disproved its rule, and none of that is expressible in the gate registry's current schema, which records a gate as PASS or FAIL and has no place for *"AN-1 and AN-2 proved falsifiable, AN-3 unreachable."* **The rule was met while the mechanism that reflects it could not reflect it.** That is not a deficiency in the rule and not a reason to delay a gate: the demonstration is the substance and the record is bookkeeping over it. **But a reader of the registry sees a green gate and nothing about which of its rules can fail** — so, until the registry can carry per-rule falsifiability, the demonstration lives in each gate's own header, where `verify-agent-naming` now carries it. **The order matters: the proof preceded the registration, and the registration did not weaken to accept the proof.**

**D-025 instance (v) — the same over-match class, now in a rename script, and it reached a build.** `scratchpad/rename-nine.mjs` replaced `/design-sync\.([a-z-]+)/g` with an exclusion list for the two audit-event names. The pattern is method-scoped in spirit and not in fact: it also matched the **import specifier** `'./agents/design-sync.js'`, rewriting it to `'./agents/sync.design-js'` — the `js` extension matched `[a-z-]+`. The build caught it immediately (`TS2307: Cannot find module`), so the cost was one round-trip. **A second miss in the same script cost more and was not caught by the build**: `FILES` listed only the two source files, so `test/design-sync-conformance.test.ts` kept eight stale ids and eleven tests failed at runtime with `AgentError: agent design-sync.idempotency: not registered`. **Both halves are the same error — a rename expressed as a text substitution over a hand-picked file list rather than as a rename of a symbol** — and they fail differently: the over-match fails loudly at compile, the under-reach fails quietly until something runs. **The exclusion list is the tell.** Writing `['completed', 'failure'].includes(rest)` is the author noticing the pattern is too broad and narrowing the *output* instead of the *pattern* — which cannot catch a case the author did not think of, and `js` was that case. **The standing lesson holds and gains a clause: more precise, not more permissive — and enumerate the files by searching for the symbol, never by recalling where it is used.**

| **D-037** | **THE CANONICAL SEQUENCE RESOLVES A REFERENCE IT NEVER READS, AND CANNOT READ.** `tenant-resolution` produces `configurationRef: string` — in the reference input, `'vault://config/t1'`, **a vault URI**. It is carried on `TenantResolutionResult` through the whole run, published in `resolutionMetadata` and in the platform event's `auditReferences`, and **dereferenced by nothing**. Measured: `ExecutionContext.configuration` carries `{ configVersion, source }` — **metadata ABOUT the configuration, not the configuration** — and **no canonical domain carries a `Record<string, string>` anywhere**. Dereferencing would need a secrets/configuration resolver, and **none of the four canonical dependencies is positioned to read a vault**: `decisionEngine`, `runtimeConnector`, `testManagementConnector`, `executionConnector`. | **DISTINCT FROM D-033, AND THE DISTINCTION IS THE ENTRY.** D-033 is *claims nobody checked* — `requirementMapping`, `certified` — where a reader existed in principle and none was written. **This is a claim nobody CAN check, because the mechanism does not exist.** No amount of attention closes it; the consumer is not late, it is absent by construction. **The harm is the reader's inference: a run carrying a `configurationRef` reads as CONFIGURED. It is not — it knows where its configuration WOULD BE.** That inference is drawn by an operator reading a platform event, a reviewer reading `resolutionMetadata`, and by anyone reasoning about whether a run honoured tenant settings. **It is also the fourth-boundary question with an unusually clean answer**: the value claims more than its producer is positioned to know, *and* no consumer is positioned to find out — both halves failing at once, which is why it survived a domain, a result type, an event and an audit trail. | Functional Testing capability — canonical runtime | **FT-001 IS CLEARED EXPLICITLY.** It declares that it resolves the tenant and names where configuration lives, and it does exactly that: `configurationRef` is correctly produced and correctly propagated. **The gap is entirely downstream** — nothing consumes it — so this is not an unmet declaration (D-024's class) and must not be recorded as one. **It is an unbuilt capability**, and the smallest honest repair is not to build the resolver but to say so: a `configurationRef` that nothing reads should be visible as such wherever it is published, or removed from the audit surface that makes it read as settled. **Sequenced with the CONFIG group of D-036** — it is that group's root, and building the dereference is what would let `configuration`, `batchSize` and `maxAttempts` reach design-synchronisation. |

| **D-036** | **THE CANONICAL RUNTIME IS A REDUCED CAPABILITY, NOT A RE-ARRANGED ONE — three independent measurements now say so.** *(i)* **D-031, fields:** the canonical `TestCase` lacks `title`, `businessGoal`, `acceptanceCriteriaCovered` and eight more that the agent-path type carries. *(ii)* **D-032, producers:** `title` and `businessGoal` have **no source at any canonical domain** — `TestScenario` carries six fields and `story.acceptanceCriteria` is a plain `string[]`. *(iii)* **The composed-form check, inputs:** design-synchronisation requires eleven inputs and the canonical composition provides **three** — and one of the eight missing, `sharedSteps`, is a **capability** rather than a field, since no canonical domain recommends shared steps at all. **Each was measured separately, from a different direction — the type, the producer, the consumer — and all three agree.** | **RECONCILED AGAINST ADR-0061 §1, AND THE DEFICIT IS INSIDE WHAT THE PARITY CLAIM COVERED.** FT-M6 measured parity for **"canonical-capability + BRIDGE"** — the composite, never canonical alone — and this register recorded that fact when `authoring-bridge.mjs` was misclassified as a retirement target (D-011). **So nothing here contradicts ADR-0061.** What it establishes is sharper and was not previously stated: **the bridge is load-bearing for CAPABILITY, not merely for packaging.** It was understood as the component that seals an `ExecutionPackage`; it is also where a story id, a story title, work item ids, design artefacts, shared-step recommendations and tenant configuration values reach design-synchronisation. **A parity claim measured over a composite says nothing about either half**, and for two sections the composite's canonical half has been treated as the surviving runtime. | Functional Testing capability — canonical runtime · ADR-0061 | **SECTION G IS BLOCKED ON THIS RECONCILIATION.** G retires the agent path, and retiring it removes the bridge — which is where these inputs come from today. **Retirement is therefore not a deletion of a superseded runtime; it is a capability decision**, and P-69.2 exists precisely to stop that being taken by accident. G now has two blockers: this, and the R-12.11/R-12.2 ADR. **Neither is F1's to resolve in passing**, and the decision is whether design-synchronisation on the canonical runtime is the same capability or a reduced one. **The eight missing inputs categorise, and the categories have different answers** — see below; they are not one ruling. |

**D-036's eight, categorised — because they do not share a disposition and one ruling over all eight would be wrong.**

| Group | Inputs | Measured | Disposition |
|---|---|---|---|
| **CONFIG** | `configuration`, `batchSize`, `maxAttempts` | **A producer plausibly exists and is never dereferenced.** `TenantResolutionResult` carries `configurationRef: string` — a **reference**, not values. **No canonical domain carries a `Record<string, string>` anywhere.** FT-001 resolves the tenant's configuration *by reference* and the sequence never reads it. | **The lightest of the four, and still not nothing.** The source is named; what is missing is the dereference. This is a plausible producer, not an absent capability — but it must be built, not assumed |
| **DEPENDENCY** | `adapter: TestDesignSyncAdapter` | real, and the only one addable by threading | **Meaningful only once the rest resolve.** Adding it alone closes one gap of nine and makes the tree look closer to a composition that cannot be reached that way |
| **CAPABILITY CONTENT, NO PRODUCER** | `storyId`, `storyTitle`, `workItemIds`, `artefacts` | **D-032's gap seen from the consumer side.** All four are authoring context — what a human named, linked and attached | **The same ruling as D-032: a SOURCE before a FIELD, and do not fabricate.** A derived story title is `objective` under another name; invented work item ids are worse |
| **MISSING CAPABILITY** | `sharedSteps` | **Categorically different from the rest.** No canonical domain recommends shared steps; there is no field to populate and no producer to build a field for | **Record as an ABSENT CAPABILITY, not an absent field.** Shared-step recommendation is analysis over an authored suite, and the canonical sequence does not perform it |
| **WRONG TYPE** | `testCases` | `CanonicalTestCase[]` versus the agent-path `TestCase[]` | **D-031, already ruled** — bridgeable through `canonicalSpecOf`, and the only one of the eleven that is |

| **D-035** | **FIFTEEN OF THE TWENTY "TRIAD AGENTS" ARE GENERATORS, NOT REVIEWERS — AND WIRING THEM INTO THE TRIAD WOULD HAVE BUILT PRODUCER-REVIEWS-SELF INTO THE GOVERNANCE TRIAD.** Found 2026-08-04 by reading what the agents DO, immediately before wiring them. The twenty were identified by their declared `stage` — 2 at `architecture-review`/`policy-review`, 18 at `guardrail-review` — and D-019, the F1 design report and its approved ruling all treated them as the triad's real reviewers. **They are not.** Measured: `test.positive` — *"Generate the happy-path scenario for each requirement"*, `outputs: Scenario[]`, `aiCapabilityClass: 'generation'`. The same for `test.negative`, `boundary`, `decision-table`, `state-transition`, `pairwise`, `error-guessing`, `exploratory`, `security`, `accessibility`, `performance-awareness`, `ai-edge-case`, `business-rule`, `data-variation`, `workflow-variation` — **fifteen generators declared at a review stage.** Three more (`duplicate-removal`, `coverage-analysis`, `certification`) are analysis over the generated set. **Only `story.review` and `story.certification` are reviews** — *"Decide whether a story is fit to author tests against"*, *"refuse on contradiction"*. | **The triad exists so that review is not performed by the reviewed, and this would have installed exactly that.** Fifteen agents that PRODUCE test scenarios, attached to the stage whose job is to review test design, reviewing the artefacts they had just generated. **The argument used to justify attaching triad agents to stage callbacks rather than to domains — that a domain-owned agent would be the producer reviewing itself — is the argument against this wiring**, and I made it in the design report while holding the list that contradicts it. **The premise was never traced: it rested on `stage`, which is a declaration, and not on `purpose` or `outputs`, which are the behaviour.** D-007's axis, committed inside an approved design report, and it would have shipped into the mechanism the platform relies on to keep producers honest. | Functional Testing capability · Platform governance | **REPORTED BEFORE WIRING. Nothing built.** The F1 design report's §3 is **withdrawn as written** — its recommendation to attach "the twenty triad agents" to the three stage callbacks is unsafe for fifteen of them. **What survives:** the *reasoning* (stages 4–6 must not be performed by the producer) is correct and is what condemns the wiring. **What is now open, and is a decision rather than a repair:** the platform has **two genuine review agents and eighteen agents that are mis-staged** — either the eighteen belong at `execution-planning` with the rest of test design and their `stage` is simply wrong (D-020's shape, at fifteen times the scale), or the triad has almost no reviewers and building it needs agents that do not exist. **Both readings are consistent with the evidence and they have opposite remedies**, which is precisely the condition under which nothing should be built. |

**D-035 TRACED, 2026-08-04 — AND THE ANSWER IS NEITHER OF THE TWO READINGS.**

**Q: were they ever INVOKED at `guardrail-review`, or was the field inert? → INVOKED, in production, deliberately.** `capability.ts:493` is the `guardrail-review` stage callback, and it calls `testOrchestrator.coordinate(...)` — the fifteen generators, plus coverage analysis, plus `test.certification`. The stage generates the scenarios, measures their coverage, and renders the Test Certified verdict, then emits `ok` or `notApplicable` on that verdict. **The stage field is not a stale label. It is load-bearing and has been for the platform's life.**

**Q: was it proximity, or intent? → INTENT, with a written reason — and the reason answers a different question than the one that matters.** `orchestrators.ts:358–370` states it plainly: *"A coverage shortfall sends work back to authoring, which is a cycle. The twelve-stage runner is forward-only over sealed results and R-12.11 forbids any path that re-enters a completed stage, so modelling this as `execution-planning -> guardrail-review` would be exactly the bypass the seal exists to prevent."* **The author saw the constraint and solved it: the author→certify→re-author loop lives inside `coordinate` so it never re-enters a sealed stage.**

**THE FINDING IS THE TRADE THAT WAS NEVER WRITTEN DOWN.** Placing the loop inside one stage satisfies **R-12.11** (no re-entering a completed stage) and violates **R-12.2**'s purpose (review not performed by the reviewed) — the producer certifies itself, at a governance-triad stage, by design. **The comment argues the first constraint at length and never mentions the second.** So this is not a mis-staged label and not an absent capability: it is **two constitutional constraints in genuine tension, resolved in favour of one, with the trade unrecorded** — which is why it survived D-019, the F1 design report and a ruling. Every reader after the author inherited a decision presented as a necessity.

**THE TRADE IS THE FINDING, NOT THE PLACEMENT.** R-12.2 was **paid for** R-12.11, and only one side of the payment appears anywhere in the text. The author saw one constraint, solved it well, and did not record the other. **A trade recorded as a trade invites scrutiny; a trade recorded as a constraint forecloses it** — and this one was recorded as a constraint, in a comment that reads as necessity. That is why it survived D-019's investigation, the F1 design report, and the ruling on it: there was nothing in the text to argue with. Every reader after the author inherited a decision wearing the appearance of the only option.

**THE PREDICTION THIS GENERATES, recorded so it can be checked.** The first instinct — *watch the governance triad* — is wrong, or at least accidental. The triad is not a place that attracts silent trades; it is **the only place where two constitutional rules currently constrain the SAME ACT**. Where that happens a trade is *necessary*, and a necessity without a record reads as design. So:

> **Look wherever two constitutional rules bear on one action. That is where a trade must be made, and therefore where an unrecorded one will be found.**

Both instances so far fit: D-019 (a triad that could not decline — R-12.2 mandates the stages, and nothing gave a stage the vocabulary to refuse) and this one (R-12.11 forbids re-entry, R-12.2 forbids self-review, one loop must satisfy both). **The prediction is falsifiable and is not yet tested: a third instance found OUTSIDE the triad, at any other point where two rules bear on one action, confirms it; a third found inside the triad with no second constraining rule refutes it.**

**Consequence for the remedy: both earlier readings are withdrawn.** Re-staging the eighteen would break the remediation loop and re-enter a sealed stage. Adding new reviewers leaves the producer still certifying itself inside `coordinate`. **The real question is the one the comment did not ask: can a coverage-remediation loop and an independent review of its output coexist under a forward-only runner?** That is an architecture question about R-12.11 versus R-12.2, not an agent-placement question, and it is the second time this section has found a constitutional tension resolved silently (D-019 was the first).

| **D-034** | **THREE AGENTS ASSUME A TEST CASE EXISTS BECAUSE, UNTIL THIS TURN, IT ALWAYS DID.** Found 2026-08-04 by the pre-port sweep, before porting, on the instruction to look for inferences that held only while the four writes returned literals. `design-sync.ts:169` sets `testCaseId: null` on a `create` decision — the case does not exist yet — and `testCaseSync` now returns that same `null` when `createTestCase` **refuses**, because a refused create produces no id. **`traceability`, `attachments` and `suiteAssignment` all take `testCaseId` and use it without checking**, so a refused create is followed by three agents writing links, attachments and a suite assignment against a case that was never created. `validation` is the exception: it opens with `if (!input.outcome.testCaseId)` and reports `test-case-exists: false`. | **LATENT, NOT LIVE, AND THE DISTINCTION IS THE POINT.** No adapter in this tree refuses, so the path is unreachable today — but it became reachable *in principle* the moment `WriteOutcome<T>` landed, and **the port would have carried it onto the canonical runtime intact**. This is the contagion corollary's second half doing exactly what it did to Section B's synchronisation branch: an inference that was SAFE while the operation could not fail, and is not safe once it can. The difference is that Section B's was found by a measurement after the fact, and this was found by a sweep before the change that would have exposed it. **`validation`'s shape is the reference pattern and was already correct** — it is the one agent written for a world where writes fail, and its `readTestCase` is also the read-back precedent Section F inherits for (b). | Functional Testing capability — design synchronisation | **Fixed as part of the port, not before it**, because the guard's correct shape depends on how the port sequences the agents: three agents skipping their work is one design, and the sequence short-circuiting after a refused create is another. **Do not guard by substituting a placeholder id** — writing links against `''` or against the canonical id would put a real link on a wrong case in a customer's tool, which is worse than not writing it. The honest options are to skip the dependent agents and report them skipped, or to stop the sequence for that case; both are visible, and both are the port's decision. **Recorded before porting so it is a decision rather than a bug found later.** |

| **D-033** | **A FIELD NOBODY READS CANNOT BE FOUND WRONG BY USE.** Two instances, four days apart, both found only while changing the field for another reason. *(i)* `requirementMapping: string` — produced once per canonical test case, **consumed nowhere** in the package or in governance. Its singular-versus-plural defect (D-029's fourth altitude) was not subtle; it was simply never exercised, because no code path ever asked it a question. *(ii)* `DomainOutput.certified` — **produced thirteen times per run and read zero times** by either canonical composition (D-012). Both fields were correct-looking, present in every result, and carried into artefacts a reader might inspect. | **D-029 is about fields that CANNOT discriminate; this is about fields where discrimination would never be TESTED, because no consumer exists to disagree.** The two compound: an unconsumed field is free to be wrong indefinitely, and it will not be caught by the mechanism that catches everything else here — contradiction with a neighbour. **This is the corollary's bounded form seen from the inside**: no consumer, no contradiction, so correctness can only come from assertion — and nobody asserts a field they do not read. **The operational consequence is the part worth keeping: an unconsumed field is not dead weight, it is an UNTESTED CLAIM.** Dead weight is inert and costs storage; an untested claim is read by humans, appears in results, and is believed. `requirementMapping` said each test case mapped to one requirement, for as long as it existed, to anyone who looked. | Platform governance — the class | **A SWEEP IS WORTH DOING AND IS NOT ATTEMPTED HERE**, because it is a different kind of work from the section it surfaced in: enumerate fields produced by a canonical domain and read by no other module, no test and no gate. Each is then one of three things, and **"a claim someone should have needed" leads, because it is the kind the evidence supports**: the consumer is the missing capability. **`certified` is the proof.** Someone eventually did need it — Section B, computing a verdict over what the domains reported about themselves — and found it had been wrong for the platform's entire history, thirteen literals per run that nobody had ever read. The other two kinds are *a claim nobody needed* (delete it) and *a claim awaiting a genuinely sequenced consumer* (assert it, per the corollary's bounded form).

**The absence of a consumer is not evidence a claim is unwanted, only that nobody has checked it.** That is the argument for sweeping rather than deleting — and it is the same argument that kept `continuousLearningAgents`, where "no consumer today" was rejected as grounds to drop a capability the platform later needs. **Both instances here turned out to be the second kind**, which is a small sample pointing the same way: an unconsumed field is more often a missing reader than a surplus writer. Not attributed to a section; it spans all six capabilities and belongs wherever field-level governance lands. |

| **D-032** | **TWO OF THE FOUR FIELDS RULED FOR ENRICHMENT HAVE NO SOURCE IN THE CANONICAL CHAIN — AND THE NARROWING IS AT DOMAIN 4, NOT DOMAIN 6.** Traced 2026-08-04 before enriching, on the instruction that a domain which never populated `title` may not have one to populate. `test-management-intelligence` builds each canonical `TestCase` from a `TestScenario`, and **`TestScenario` carries exactly six fields**: `id`, `kind`, `objective`, `precondition`, `expectedOutcome`, `traceabilityRef`. Upstream of that, `story.acceptanceCriteria` is `readonly string[]` — plain strings. **`title` and `businessGoal` exist nowhere in the canonical sequence**, at any domain, in any form. `test-management-intelligence` did not omit them; it never received them, because `test-design-intelligence` constructs scenarios from an acceptance criterion and a kind, and an acceptance criterion is a sentence rather than a titled artefact. **The other two ARE reachable:** `requirementIds` from `design.requirementId` (honestly plural-capable even though this composition supplies one), and `acceptanceCriteriaCovered` from the scenario's `expectedOutcome`, which for a positive scenario **is the acceptance criterion verbatim**. | **Adding `title` and `businessGoal` to the canonical `TestCase` would create two fields that can only be populated by inventing them**, which is the fabrication this section has spent its length removing — and it would do so in the domain whose output is published into a customer's system of record. A `title` derived as `'verify: ' + objective` is not a title; it is `objective` wearing a second name, and a reviewer reading a published suite would take it for authored intent. **This is D-013's shape moved upstream:** a field whose type promises content its producers cannot supply. The correct reading is that the ruling's premise was right about the REQUIREMENT — FT-009 publishes to a tool that needs a title — and the deficit is one domain earlier than the port made it look. | Functional Testing capability — canonical domains 3 and 4 | **ENRICH WITH THE TWO THAT HAVE A SOURCE; REPORT THE OTHER TWO RATHER THAN FABRICATE THEM.** `requirementIds` replaces `requirementMapping` and `acceptanceCriteriaCovered` is derived — both honest today. **`title` and `businessGoal` need a source before they need a field**, and the source is `TestScenario` and `StoryIntelligenceResult`, not `TestManagementResult`. **Two candidate routes, both upstream, neither an author's call:** `test-design-intelligence` composes a title when it constructs a scenario, since it is the domain that knows the criterion and the kind and is where naming would legitimately happen; or the requirement model carries titles and business goals from the start, which is a change to what a caller supplies. **For publication specifically there is a third, and it is not an enrichment at all:** the tool requires a title, so the canonical-to-`TestCaseSpec` mapping may carry `objective` into `TestCaseSpec.title` at the publication boundary — an explicit mapping recorded where publication happens, rather than a canonical field that pretends the runtime knows something it does not. **RULED 2026-08-04 and NOT YET APPLIED, for a stated reason:** the existing `specOf` takes the AGENT-PATH `TestCase`, which has a real authored `title`, so there is nothing to map there. The mapping belongs at the canonical-to-spec boundary **that the port creates**, and forcing it into `specOf` today would put a fallback where a real value already exists. |

**D-032 IS A CAPABILITY GAP OF THE SEQUENCE, NOT A TYPE GAP — ruled 2026-08-04, and the distinction is the whole entry.**

A type gap is repaired by adding a field. **There is no domain positioned to populate these two.** `TestScenario` carries six fields; `story.acceptanceCriteria` is a plain `string[]`; nothing anywhere in `CANONICAL_DOMAIN_SEQUENCE` holds a title or a business rationale in any form. **The canonical runtime cannot express a titled test case or the reason a case matters, and no domain is positioned to produce one.**

**Same class as D-015's 109 declared-but-unevidenced certification criteria and D-012's missing certification step: declared capability with no machinery.** In each, the artefact that should carry the thing exists and the mechanism that would produce it does not — which is why each was invisible until something asked for the output.

**The consequence against the canon, stated plainly.** **FT-009 publishes to a tool that requires authored intent, and the sequence produces steps and expected results only.** A published suite from the canonical runtime tells a reader what will be checked and never why it matters or what it is called. That is not a formatting difference; it is the difference between a suite a customer's QA lead can review and a list of assertions.

**The two upstream routes are SECTION F's decision, attributed there and not taken here.** Naming a scenario is *authoring* work, and `test-design-intelligence` is where `GherkinAuthoringAgent` and `TestClassificationTaggingAgent` land in the agent port. Whether test-design composes a title at scenario construction — it knows the criterion and the kind, and naming would legitimately happen there — or the requirement model carries titles and goals from the start, which changes what a caller supplies, is a decision that belongs with the agents that would do the naming. **`businessGoal` stays a stated upstream gap and is deliberately not closed cheaply**; there is no derivation of it that would not be an invention.

**One adjacent fact, found while replacing it: `requirementMapping` was read by NOTHING.** Produced once per test case, consumed nowhere in the package or in governance. Its singular-versus-plural defect was therefore invisible not because it was subtle but because **the field carried no information to anyone** — the same shape as D-012's `certified` flag, produced thirteen times and consumed zero. A field nobody reads cannot be found wrong by use.

| **D-031** | **TWO DIFFERENT TYPES ARE BOTH CALLED `TestCase` IN ONE PACKAGE, AND THE CANONICAL ONE CARRIES STRICTLY LESS THAN THE DESIGN-SYNC AGENTS REQUIRE.** Found 2026-08-04 by the pre-port shape trace, before adapting either side. `domains/test-management-intelligence.ts:34` defines the canonical `TestCase`; `model.ts:423` defines the agent-path `TestCase`; `agents/design-sync.ts` imports the **latter**. They are not variants of one shape — **the same field name means different things and several have no counterpart at all**: `requirementIds: readonly string[]` versus `requirementMapping: string` (plural versus singular), `preconditions: readonly string[]` versus `precondition: string`, `testData: Record<string, string>` versus `testData: readonly string[]`, `postConditions: readonly string[]` versus `postcondition: string`. **Absent from the canonical type entirely:** `title`, `scenarioId`, `businessGoal`, `acceptanceCriteriaCovered`, `businessRuleIds`, `dependencies`, `assumptions`, `environment`, `validation`, `cleanup`, `testDataSets`. `tags` is the one non-trivial field that matches. | **The port cannot proceed without a ruling, and BOTH available shortcuts are prohibited.** Reshaping the agents to consume the canonical type would silently discard `title`, `businessGoal`, `acceptanceCriteriaCovered` and `testDataSets` — the fields that make a published test case readable to a customer, and the difference between a suite that documents intent and one that lists steps. Widening the canonical `TestCase` to carry them would import the agent path's model into the runtime that survives retirement, which is the opposite of a port. **Neither is a mapping problem; the two types encode different amounts of knowledge**, and the deficit is on the side that survives. **The duplicate name is its own hazard**: `TestCase` resolves to different types in two files of one package, so a reader — or an author writing an import — cannot tell from the name which they have. | Functional Testing capability — the port · Platform governance (the duplicate name) | **REPORTED, NOT RESOLVED, per the standing instruction not to reshape either side silently.** Three candidate resolutions, none an author's call: **(a)** the canonical `TestCase` gains the missing fields because they are genuinely capability content the canonical runtime should carry — which is enrichment of the survivor rather than accommodation of the agent, and must be argued on that basis, not on the port's convenience; **(b)** the design-sync agents are ported against a REDUCED spec, with every discarded field named and the loss recorded — honest, and a visible capability reduction rather than a silent one; **(c)** the port is deferred and P-69.2 stays open for design-synchronisation, which is the status quo and should be chosen deliberately if it is chosen. **The duplicate name should be resolved regardless of which**, because the next author to write `import type { TestCase }` has a coin-flip. |

| **D-030** | **`SyncReport.status` CANNOT BE ANYTHING BUT `SUCCESS`, AND THE AGENT THAT PRODUCES IT DECLARES OTHERWISE.** Found 2026-08-04 by the pre-port sweep of the nine `designSyncAgents`, before any porting. `report`'s status is `failures.length === 0 ? 'SUCCESS' : succeeded.length === 0 ? 'FAILED' : 'PARTIAL'`, where `failures` = failed write outcomes + invalid validations. **Neither input can be non-empty in this tree.** *(i)* A write outcome is `succeeded: false` only if `createTestCase`/`updateTestCase` throws; both return `SyncedTestCase` unconditionally and the reference adapter never throws, so the `try/catch` at `agents/design-sync.ts:275` guards a case that cannot occur. *(ii)* A validation is invalid only if a read-back check fails; the reference adapter stores exactly what it was told, so every check passes by construction. **`PARTIAL` and `FAILED` are therefore unreachable, and the two conformance tests assert only `status SUCCESS`** — the negative path is neither reachable nor asserted (D-026's shape). The agent's own `failureHandling` reads *"A run with any failure is PARTIAL or FAILED and names every failure"* — **a fifth `failureHandling` that was unimplementable when written** (D-024's class). | **This is the whole register in one field.** The status is an aggregate over inputs that cannot vary (D-029), asserted only in the value it can take (D-026/D-027), declared to do something its machinery forbids (D-024), and reported to a human as the headline of a synchronisation run. **It is also the most consequential of the five**, because `SUCCESS` on a design-synchronisation run is what tells a customer their certified suite is in their tool — and it is the one value the field can hold. | Functional Testing capability — design synchronisation | **Fixed as part of the port, not before it**, because the repair is the same decision as `createTestCase`/`updateTestCase`'s payload-carrying union: once a write can report refusal without throwing, `succeeded` discriminates, `failures` can be non-empty, and `PARTIAL`/`FAILED` become reachable. **The `try/catch` stays** — a thrown transport error and a refused write are different facts, and collapsing them would repeat the not-applicable/refused conflation ADR-0071 removed. **Two further literal-success operations found in the same sweep and NOT covered by ADR-0073:** `createSharedStep` and `createSharedParameter`, over which `sharedAssets.created` counts attempts (D-029 again). They are in the port's scope. |

| **D-029** | **WHERE A FIELD IS A TYPE-LEVEL LITERAL, EVERY AGGREGATE OVER IT IS A COUNT OF ATTEMPTS WEARING THE NAME OF OUTCOMES.** Third instance in two sections. *(i)* `agents/design-sync.ts` — `if (adapter.linkWorkItem(…).linked) links += 1`, over a `{ linked: true }` return, so `links` was the call count. The agent's own `failureHandling` says the census *"reports what the tool accepted, never what was attempted"* — **it reported precisely what was attempted, and said so in the same file.** *(ii)* `defectManagement.eligibleCount` — `defects.filter((d) => d.eligible).length` over a hardcoded `eligible: true`, so it was `defects.length` (D-013 ii). *(iii)* `synchronisation.publishedObjects` — every entry constructed `status: 'published'`, so the count of published objects was the count of SUBMITTED ones, which is why a zero-published condition could not fire and had to be withdrawn. | **A count is the most persuasive shape a measurement can take, and the least self-describing.** `links: 3` reads as three links; nothing in the number says whether the tool agreed. The aggregate inherits its predicate's blindness while shedding every trace of it — `filter(…).length` over an undiscriminating field produces an integer that looks measured, is reported to executives, and is consumed downstream as fact. All three instances fed a summary a human reads: the link census, `eligibleDefects` in the executive summary, and `publishedCount` in the synchronisation metadata. **This is D-027 one step further out again**: a property that cannot fail, an assertion that cannot discriminate, and now an aggregate that cannot vary — the same defect at three altitudes, each less visible than the last, because a number carries no evidence of its own derivation. | Platform governance — the class · Functional Testing capability (all three instances) | **All three fixed by making the underlying field discriminate rather than by changing the aggregate** — which is the correct direction: a count over an honest field needs no repair, and a repaired count over a literal would still be a literal. `links` now counts `published`, `eligibleCount` counts `duplicateAssessment === 'unique'`, and `publishedObjects` carries per-object `status`. **The generalisable check, and it is mechanisable where D-027's was not:** for every `filter(…).length` or boolean tally in the tree, does the predicate's field have more than one reachable value? A field with one reachable value makes its aggregate a constant, and a constant reported as a measurement is the failure this register keeps finding. |

| **D-028** | **THE DESIGN-SYNCHRONISATION SPI HAS THE SAME DEFECT ADR-0072 JUST REMOVED FROM ITS SIBLING — and ADR-0072 did not touch it.** `TestDesignSyncAdapter` (`capability-framework/src/adapters.ts`) returns type-level literals from every write: `linkWorkItem → { linked: true }`, `applyClassification → { applied: true }`, `assignToSuite → { assigned: true }`, `createTestCase`/`updateTestCase → SyncedTestCase`, `attachDesignArtefact → { attachmentId }`. **Not one can report that the customer's tool refused.** ADR-0072 widened `TestManagementAdapter` and `ExecutionAdapter` because those were the SPIs Section C's publication semantics ran through; this is the third publication SPI and was out of that change's scope. **An inference is already living on it:** `agents/design-sync.ts:302–307` counts links with `if (adapter.linkWorkItem(…).linked) links += 1`, and since `linked` is the literal `true`, **the count is always the number of calls** — a tally that cannot distinguish a linked work item from an attempted one. | **FIFTH INSTANCE OF THE TRUTHFUL-NEGATIVE PATTERN, AND IT WAS FOUND WHERE THE COROLLARY SAID TO LOOK.** The instruction was to watch for the corollary's second half while porting the nine `designSyncAgents` — for inferences that only held while publication could not fail — and the inference was in the link tally, on an SPI one layer beside the one just repaired. **The port would have carried it onto the canonical runtime intact.** Porting nine agents onto an SPI that cannot express refusal is the ADR-0071 sequencing argument exactly: capability built on a channel that discards the signal, **worse than not porting, because it would look complete**. | **Platform — `@dbiz/capability-framework`** (the SPI) · Functional Testing capability (the port) | **THE PLATFORM ALREADY CONTAINS THE RIGHT PATTERN, IN THIS SAME FILE, AND IT SHOULD BE READ BEFORE THE SPI IS WIDENED.** `design-sync.ts`'s validation phase calls `readTestCase` and checks what the tool actually holds — `linkedWorkItems`, `tags`, `suiteIds`, `attachmentIds` — rather than trusting the write's return value. Its own comment says it: *"read back, so validation observes the tool rather than trusting the write."* **That is a stronger guarantee than a return code**, because it survives a lying adapter, and the publication path has no equivalent. **Two candidate repairs, and the choice is a decision, not an author's preference:** (a) widen `TestDesignSyncAdapter` to `PublicationOutcome` as ADR-0072 did for its siblings — consistent, mechanical, and still trusts the adapter's self-report; (b) adopt read-back validation for publication too, which is what design-sync already does and what a real ADO connector makes possible. **RULED 2026-08-04: (a) NOW, (b) SEQUENCED TO SECTION F**, with (b)'s three reasons recorded at the SPI's own decision site rather than only here — it cannot be proven in this tree because every adapter tells the truth by construction and the failure mode needs a real connector; the two COMPOSE, so the outcome type becomes a corroborating signal rather than being replaced; and it is declaration-versus-behaviour work, which is Section F's class. **(a) landed behaviour-neutral and bounded**: four signatures, one reference adapter, one agent file, no other capability touched. **`createTestCase`/`updateTestCase` are NOT widened and it is stated** — they return a `SyncedTestCase` payload, so expressing refusal needs a payload-carrying union rather than this one, which is a different decision belonging with the port that consumes them. Their failure stays inexpressible until then. |

**D-028's port surfaced FOUR MORE D-024 INSTANCES, in one file, before a single agent was ported — which is what looking for them first was for.** Every one of these `failureHandling` declarations was **unimplementable when written**, because the SPI it described could not fail:

| Agent | Declared | What the code did |
|---|---|---|
| traceability | *"the census reports what the tool accepted, **never what was attempted**"* | counted attempts — `linked` was the literal `true` |
| attachments | *"an artefact that cannot be attached is counted as not attached"* | counted all attempted — `attachmentId` always returned |
| suite assignment | *"an assignment that fails is reported unassigned"* | `assigned` was the literal `true` |
| classification | reported `applied` | `applied` was the literal `true` |

**The first is the sharpest thing in this register.** It does not merely overstate; it names the exact failure mode it exhibits, in the same file, one line above the code that exhibits it. Nobody wrote it carelessly — it describes what the author intended and what the SPI made impossible. **All four are now true**, wired as part of (a), and the wiring is behaviour-neutral because the reference adapters accept everything. **They are wired rather than deferred, unlike ADR-0072's, because these belong to the Functional Testing capability** — the ownership argument that deferred Dev-Change's and Inverse-Flow's applies here in reverse.

| **D-027** | **A TEST CAN ENCODE THE DEFECT IT APPEARS TO GUARD AGAINST.** Two instances, both found 2026-08-04 by making `defect-management`'s `eligible` field discriminate for the first time. *(i)* `assert.ok(r.defects.length > 0 && r.eligibleCount === r.defects.length)` — the identity held **only because `eligible` was the literal `true` on every defect** (D-013 ii). The test asserted the very equality that made the field meaningless, and it failed the moment the field started to carry information. *(ii)* `assert.ok(r.defects.every((d) => d.eligible && d.severity === 'medium' && d.priority === 'p2'))` — three properties in one assertion, one of which could not vary, so **the conjunction looked like triple coverage and was double coverage plus a constant**. | **AN ASSERTION OVER AN UNDISCRIMINATING FIELD LOOKS LIKE COVERAGE IN EXACTLY THE WAY THE FIELD LOOKS LIKE DATA — and both are invisible to inspection of the result.** A green test over a literal is indistinguishable, in every artefact anyone reads, from a green test over a computed value; the test name says what it *intends* to check, the assertion says what it *does*, and nothing reconciles them. Worse than an absent test, because it occupies the space where the real one would go: a reviewer asking *"is eligibility covered?"* finds a passing test named for it. **This is how D-013's undiscriminating fields survived — they were not merely unnoticed, they were ASSERTED**, and the assertions were counted among the passing. | Platform governance — the class · Functional Testing capability (the two instances) | **THE CORRECT REPAIR IS TO THE ASSERTION, NOT THE CODE, and it was applied that way**: both now assert the property rather than the identity — `eligibleCount` equals the count of `duplicateAssessment === 'unique'`, and severity/priority are asserted without a constant riding along. Reverting the field to a literal to make the tests pass again would have been the available alternative and the wrong one. **The class is R-13.7's subject displaced one level out: R-13.7 asks whether a PROPERTY can fail; this asks whether an ASSERTION can distinguish.** A property that cannot fail and an assertion that cannot discriminate are the same defect at different altitudes, and neither is visible from the pass/fail result. **The generalisable check, not yet mechanised:** for every assertion over a boolean or an enum, does the fixture set produce more than one value of it? Where it does not, the assertion is a constant wearing a test. |

| **D-026** | **A NEGATIVE-PATH VARIANT WITH NO ASSERTION ON ITS OUTPUT IS ONE REFACTOR AWAY FROM EXERCISING NOTHING — and that is exactly what happened.** Variant F (`referenceDependencies('F')`) exists for one purpose: to drive the canonical domains' failure branches so they are proven to fire rather than assumed to. **Nothing in the suite asserts WHICH findings it produces.** The conformance tests run it and assert structural properties; `canonical-failure-conditions.test.ts` asserts the four Pass 2 conditions against their own named inputs and does not cover variant F's set. So when ADR-0072 made `publishTests` return one outcome per test, `synchronisation`'s `acknowledged < submitted` branch became unreachable and **variant F silently stopped producing a synchronisation finding, with 1,427 tests passing**. The variant still ran. It still failed four components. It simply proved one thing fewer, and nothing was watching the count. | **This is the vacuous-green class (D-015) displaced one level: not a property that cannot fail, but a FIXTURE whose purpose is unasserted.** A negative-path variant is evidence only in combination with a statement of what it should evidence; alone it is a code path that executes. The harm is specific to how this programme works — every failure branch in the canonical domains was justified by "variant F fires it", and that justification decays silently, because a branch dying leaves no artefact. **It is also the mechanism by which the contagion corollary's second half bites:** an honest layer removes an inference, the inference was load-bearing for a fixture's coverage, and nothing reports the loss. | Functional Testing capability — governance scenarios | **FIXED AS PART OF SECTION C ruling (b), not deferred:** assert variant F's expected findings **by domain and category**, so that a branch dying is a test failure rather than a silence. The assertion must name the set, not its size — a count would have passed here too, since the coverage finding remained. **The general rule this establishes: a fixture built to exercise a negative path carries an assertion about which negatives it produces, in the same change that introduces it.** Retrofitting it to variant F is the immediate work; the same question applies to any future variant, and to `partialTestManagementConnector` and `failingRuntimeConnector` individually rather than only in combination. |

| **D-025** | **AN OVER-PERMISSIVE PATTERN MATCHES A DIFFERENT THING THAT LEGITIMATELY HAS THE SAME SHAPE — twice, in two different tools, four days apart in one session.** *(i) The closure-package guard.* Written to fail on an unparseable debt row, its first rule was *"begins with `|` and mentions `D-nnn` anywhere"*. It fired on its first real exercise against a **narrative table inside §1 whose rows cite debt ids in their prose** — rows that are not debt rows at all. *(ii) The ADR-0072 migration.* A regex for `{ linked: true }`, intended for `TestManagementAdapter.linkTraceability`, rewrote **`linkWorkItemTraceability` and `linkRequirement`** — different SPIs that return that shape correctly and were not part of the change. Two files had to be reverted. **A third, milder recurrence in the same migration:** two `publishTests` rules both matched the same line, wrapping a `PublicationOutcome` inside a `PublicationOutcome`. | **The shape is not evidence of the meaning, and a pattern only ever sees the shape.** `{ linked: true }` is the correct return of three different operations; a line beginning `\|` and containing `D-019` is a debt row in one table and prose in another. Each over-match is a **false positive in a mechanism built to prevent false confidence**, which is the worst place for one: (i) would have blocked emission on a correct register, and (ii) silently changed code outside the change's scope — **the more dangerous of the two, because a compiler error is loud and a wrong-but-valid rewrite is not.** Instance (ii) was caught only because the rewritten call sites belonged to interfaces that still expected the old shape; had those SPIs been migrated in the same pass, the edit would have compiled and shipped. | Programme process | **THE LESSON HELD BOTH TIMES, AND IT IS THE ENTRY'S POINT: MAKE THE PATTERN MORE PRECISE, NOT MORE PERMISSIVE.** (i) was repaired by requiring the identifier in the **first cell** rather than anywhere in the line — which still fails on both known omissions and no longer fires on prose. (ii) was repaired by **exact, method-scoped strings** rather than a shape regex. The tempting repair in both cases is the opposite one — relax the rule until the false positive stops — and that would have restored the original defect in exchange for silence, which is how a guard becomes decoration. **Operationally: when a pattern over-matches, the correct response is to add the discriminator the pattern was missing, and if no discriminator exists, to stop using a pattern.** Recorded together rather than as two incidents because a single occurrence reads as carelessness and two in one session read as what they are — a property of matching on shape. |

| **D-022** | **A DESIGN REPORT'S BLAST-RADIUS ESTIMATE WAS WRONG BY AN ORDER OF MAGNITUDE, AND REVIEW DID NOT CATCH IT — IMPLEMENTATION DID.** The ADR-0071 design report stated the refusal primitive's surface as *"`StageResult` consumers reading `applicable`/`notApplicableReason`: **one** — `certification.ts:98,104`"*, derived by grep and reasoning about how the type is used. The measured surface was **24 typed call sites across five packages, plus 4 untyped governance scenarios the compiler cannot see** — 28 in total. The estimate was not careless: the reasoning behind it was correct, in that the type *is* consumed almost entirely through its emitter. What it missed is that a type's **shape** is asserted far more widely than its **behaviour** is consumed, and test files assert shape. The error surfaced the moment the field was removed and the build enumerated every reader; it was never going to surface from reading. | **The report was reviewed and ruled on with the wrong number in it, and nothing in the review process could have found the right one.** A blast-radius estimate is a claim about code that only the compiler can settle, and a design report is precisely the artefact produced before the compiler has been asked. This is the same axis as D-018 — a measurement encoding a question its inputs had already answered wrongly — but applied to a *plan* rather than a result, and it has a different remedy: not a better estimate, but a shorter interval between estimating and measuring. **It is a concrete argument for build-and-measure over estimate-and-approve**, and it is worth recording that the argument arrived as evidence rather than as preference. | Programme process | **Do not respond by demanding more rigorous estimates.** A more careful grep would have found more sites and still not all of them, and the extra care would have been spent on a number the build produces for free. The efficiency amendment already carries the correct response — where a design is ruled, design and build land together, so the estimate is superseded by the measurement within the same turn rather than standing as an approved figure for the interval between. **What an estimate should carry instead is how it was derived and what would settle it**, so a reader knows whether they are looking at a measurement or a projection: the ADR-0071 report said "measured" over a table that was partly reasoned. **SECOND INSTANCE, ADR-0072, and it is the more instructive of the two** — see below. |

**D-022 instance (ii) — the same error with the correct conclusion already written down.** The Section C design report stated that the publication-SPI change *"changes `@dbiz/capability-framework`'s adapter contract, which every capability's connectors implement — a second platform-wide change in one session"*. **That sentence is right.** The report then did not carry it into any expectation of the consumer surface, and the measured migration was 5 signatures, 7 adapter implementations, 5 test-fixture adapter sets and 9 consumer sites — enough that the first attempt was backed out mid-way rather than rushed.

**The two instances differ in a way that matters.** In (i) the reasoning was wrong — the surface was believed small because the type is consumed through its emitter. In (ii) **the reasoning was right and was not converted into a number**, so nothing in the report was falsifiable and nothing was falsified. A qualitative "platform-wide" reads as a caveat; "28 sites" reads as a plan. **An estimate that cannot be wrong is not an estimate**, which is R-13.7's clause 1 applied to planning rather than to properties — and it is why the remedy is not more rigour but a shorter interval before the compiler answers.

**Together they are the argument for build-and-measure over estimate-and-approve**, and the argument is stronger for having arrived twice, in opposite failure modes, within one session.

| **D-021** | **TWO CERTIFICATION MECHANISMS RETURN OPPOSITE ANSWERS FOR THE SAME RUN, AND NO ARTEFACT SAYS WHICH GOVERNS.** Measured 2026-08-04 on the canonical runtime, reference input, one execution: `certify()` returns `certified: true, firstRefusal: null` while `executiveReporting.certificationVerdict` returns `NOT CERTIFIED` with one negative finding. **They are NOT redundant.** The stage verdict gates *whether the run may proceed* — `runtime-entry-point-bridge.ts:103` throws on `!certification.certified`. The domain verdict states *what the run concluded* — FT-037, computed from the run-state record. Two different questions, correctly given two different answers. **The defect is that nothing anywhere says so.** No ADR, no contract, no comment and no artefact records that these are distinct questions, in what order they apply, or what a consumer holding both should do. `RuntimeExecutionOutcome` exposes `certification.certified` (the domain verdict, since FT-037) while the stage verdict silently gates the same call — so a caller sees one, is governed by the other, and can distinguish neither. | **The disagreement was created by making something correct.** Before FT-037 both mechanisms were unconditionally positive: `certify()` cannot say no (D-019) and every domain hardcoded `certified: true` (D-012). Two mechanisms that always agree because neither can dissent are indistinguishable from one mechanism, and the ambiguity was invisible. Giving d13 a verdict that *can* be negative did not introduce the ambiguity — **it was the first thing capable of exposing it**, which is the same shape as D-014's coverage finding appearing on the first run that could report it. **The consumer-facing cost is concrete:** a run that concluded NOT CERTIFIED proceeds, dispatches an ExecutionPackage and returns an outcome, because the mechanism that gates proceeding cannot express the conclusion the other mechanism reached. **`firstRefusal` is a field named for the intended design rather than the implemented one, and has been since it was written** — it is the first non-certified verdict, and today the only reachable values are *did not run* and *not applicable*, neither of which is a refusal. | Platform — `@dbiz/capability-framework` · Functional Testing capability | **Recorded BEFORE the refusal primitive lands, deliberately, so that ADR-0071 cannot read as having addressed it.** The primitive (D-019) makes reconciliation *possible* — a domain-level negative finding could reach the stage verdict — but **it does not perform the reconciliation, and adopting it changes no run's outcome.** The decision still owed: which mechanism governs, whether a domain's negative finding SHOULD block proceeding or only be reported, and what `RuntimeExecutionOutcome` must expose so a consumer can tell the two apart. **Do not resolve it by deleting one.** Both questions are real; a capability that could not say *"I ran, and here is what I concluded"* separately from *"I was permitted to run"* would have lost information, which is the failure mode this register keeps recording. |

| **D-020** | **THE AGENT NAMED FOR THE PLATFORM'S CERTIFICATION STEP DOES NOT RUN AT THE CERTIFICATION STAGE.** `governance.final-certification`, in `agents/review-board.ts`, declares `stage: 'reporting'`. The framework's twelve stages include `certification`, the canonical runner implements it (`canonical-runner-capability.ts:184`), and `certify()` maps the `release-certified` gate to it. Measured across the catalogue: **2 agents declare `stage: 'certification'` and neither is this one**, while all 16 `reviewBoardAgents` — every agent named `governance.review.*` plus `governance.consistency` and `governance.final-certification` — declare `reporting`. | **A declaration read by a reviewer, a port, or a gate as *"certification is performed by an agent at the certification stage"* is false, and nothing reports it.** This is D-007's axis with the same shape as instance (iii), the healing plane/stage drift: the name and the declared stage disagree, no gate compares them, and the mismatch survives because both halves are individually plausible. The immediate cost is a porting hazard rather than a runtime one — **`RETIREMENT_RESOLUTION_REGISTER.md` §6 lands these sixteen at `reporting` because that is what they declare**, which is correct against the declaration and wrong against the name, and a later reader will reasonably ask why the final-certification agent reports instead of certifying. | Functional Testing capability — agent declarations | **Do not silently re-stage it as part of the Section F port.** Moving `governance.final-certification` to `stage: 'certification'` is a one-line change and would be the wrong first move: the FT-037 verdict is already computed by `executive-reporting` at the certification stage (`canonical-runner-capability.ts:184–190`), so re-staging the agent without deciding what it contributes would put two things at one stage with no stated division of work — the duplication CHARTER §4 forbids, created while fixing a naming drift. **Decide what the agent certifies that d13 does not, then re-stage it.** If the answer is nothing, the honest outcome is that the agent is redundant and says so, not that it is quietly moved. WP5 (agent naming) is the natural home; it is sequenced after retirement. |

**D-015 instance (iii) — PRESENTATIONAL EMPHASIS IN A MACHINE-READ REGISTER SILENTLY DELETES THE ENTRY IT WAS MEANT TO EMPHASISE, and it fails in both directions at once.** The general form, stated first because it outlives both incidents: where one field carries a human-readable marker and a machine-readable identifier, **strengthening the first destroys the second** — and neither artefact shows it. The table reads as *more* correct, because the emphasis is there; the JSON reads as *complete*, because a skipped row leaves no hole. There is no signal anywhere that a row went missing.

**THE ASYMMETRY IS THE FINDING, NOT EITHER INCIDENT.** Both rows failed the same parser for the same reason. Only one was caught, and *which* one was caught was decided entirely by whether its absence looked wrong:

| | Row | Absent from `openDebt` | Detected? | Because |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *(i)* | `\| **D-019** · **HIGHEST SEVERITY** \|` | hours | **Yes** — from the `baseline.json` diff showing `-"D-019" +"D-020"` where both belonged | a highest-severity entry going missing **looks wrong** |
| *(ii)* | `\| **D-014 — RESOLVED 2026-08-04: …** \|` | **since the day it was written** | **No** — surfaced only when the parser was made to fail | a resolved entry absent from an open-debt list **looks right** |

**A silent omission that produces the desired output is undetectable by inspecting the output.** Inspection can only ever catch the omissions that look wrong, which is a filter on *appearance*, not on *correctness* — and it selects against exactly the omissions that will survive longest. That is a stronger argument for failing on an unparseable row than "a parser that skips is a gate that cannot fail", because it holds even for a reader who checks the output carefully every time.

**Repaired at the parser, not only at the two rows: a parser that SKIPS is a gate that cannot fail on the thing it reads** (CHARTER R-13.7). Any line in §1 that looks like a debt row — begins with `|` and names a `D-nnn` — must now parse, or emission **fails and names the row**. Proved by running it before fixing anything: it failed, naming D-014. Both cells were then corrected and `openDebt` went from 16 entries to 17.

**OPEN QUESTION, RAISED AND DELIBERATELY NOT FIXED — `openDebt` reflects membership of the §1 table, not resolution status.** D-005 and D-014 both carry RESOLVED in their text and both appear in the closure package's open-debt list. That was true before the parser change and was merely hidden behind a parse failure; fixing the parser made it visible, not worse. **Filtering on the word "RESOLVED" would replace one silent omission with another** — a heuristic over prose, dropping rows on a keyword, is the mechanism that just cost two entries. **Register hygiene is the ruling authority's**, and the question is whether resolved entries move out of §1 rather than whether the emitter should learn to guess. Raised here; not settled here.

## STANDING RULE — A CONTROL THAT NEEDS AN INVARIANT RELAXED IN ORDER TO RUN IS ARGUING WITH ITS OWN SUBJECT

**Ruled 2026-08-06. The instance is small and the shape is not: the gate written to measure the
deployment could not itself live in the plane it measures, and the allowlist would have let it.**

`verify-deployment-currency` was written to answer D-144's *"is this the commit the deployment
serves?"*. Its first version called `fetch`. **`verify-intelligence-plane-egress` flagged it in the
same run that produced it** — **EG-2, `no Intelligence-Plane source opens an outbound connection`**
(R-3.2, R-6.3, R-14.16).

> **THE ALLOWLIST WOULD HAVE TAKEN IT, AND THAT IS WHAT MAKES THIS WORTH RECORDING.** EG-2 has a
> governed exception mechanism — file, host, rationale, with EG-3 asserting no entry goes stale. One
> justified entry and the gate runs. **Nothing would have gone red, and the invariant would have
> acquired a hole on behalf of a control that was twenty minutes old.**

**A control exists to assert a property of the system. When it cannot run without the system giving
up a property, the control has become a second thing competing with the first** — and the exception
is always locally reasonable, because the control's author is the one writing the rationale.

### The independent argument, which does not depend on the invariant at all

> **Every other gate in this suite is DETERMINISTIC AND OFFLINE. That one was neither.**

It could not run in an offline CI, and **a transport blip and real drift arrived at the same
function** — discriminable only by code that would not need to exist if the gate did not dial. That
is the reach-versus-refusal rule appearing *inside* an instrument rather than in the system it
measures.

### The repair, and it is the SECOND time this exact split has been the right answer

**The OBSERVATION became an input.** Whoever can reach the deployment records what it said — one
documented line of `curl`, outside the plane — and the gate decides what that means. **A missing
observation FAILS**, because failing to look is the case it exists to catch.

> **SEPARATING OBSERVATION FROM JUDGEMENT HAS NOW BEEN THE RIGHT ANSWER TO TWO UNRELATED PROBLEMS,
> AND THAT IS WHY IT IS RECORDED AS A RULE RATHER THAN A FIX.**
>
> · **`undistributed()` / `publishWorkPaths()`** — split so that *"who cannot discover the
>   exchange?"* can be asked **by a caller with no authority to emit**, and asked without writing.
> · **`verify-deployment-currency`** — split so the judging half needs **no egress and no network**,
>   and stays deterministic.
>
> **Different motivations — authority in one case, determinism in the other — and the same shape.
> The party that may ASK is not always the party that may ACT, and the party that JUDGES needs
> neither capability.**

---

## STANDING RULE — A GREEN SUITE OVER AN UNPUSHED COMMIT MEASURES A PRIVATE ARTEFACT

**Ruled 2026-08-06, above the instance ([`D-144`](TECHNICAL_DEBT.md)), because the instance is the
sixth of its class in this programme and the first where what was lost is CAPABILITY rather than
history.**

> **CHARTER §3 step 3 makes git history the FACT against a state file's CLAIM. This is the case that
> rule does not reach: the history and the claim AGREED, and both were about something other than what
> serves traffic.** The missing question is **"is this the commit the deployment serves?"**, and no
> instrument in this programme asks it.

**Build, suite, governance, every gate — all read the WORKING TREE.** They are correct about it. Their
correctness is the problem: a green measurement of an artefact nobody can reach is indistinguishable,
in every register this programme keeps, from a green measurement of the running system.

### The check that was absent, and why the CHEAP version of it is a trap

`git rev-parse origin/main` against `HEAD` is one line, costs nothing, and **would have caught this
instance** (`0 31`). It is still the wrong gate on its own, and the reason is this programme's own rule:

> **CHARTER §17.1.1, the subject-removal test — of every property a control asserts, ask: if its
> SUBJECT were removed, would this property turn RED or GREEN?** A `HEAD == origin/main` check
> **GOES GREEN WHEN NOTHING IS DEPLOYED AT ALL.** It is satisfied *by* the absence of a deployment.
> It measures **pushed**, and pushed is not served.

**Two further defects in the cheap version, both familiar:**
- It reads the **remote-tracking ref**, which is only as fresh as the last `fetch` — a check that
  cannot tell a stale answer from a current one, which is D-107's class.
- A divergence is **not automatically a fault.** Work in progress is normal. What is a fault is an
  **unqualified "IS MOUNTED" written over a divergence**, and that is a property of the state file,
  not of the git graph.

### What the sufficient check needs, and the good news is that half of it exists

**The running system must report the commit it was built from.** The pipeline **already has the
value** — `azure-pipelines/deploy-api.yml:49` sets `TAG="${BUILD_SOURCEVERSION:0:7}"` and tags the
image with it. **The process does not expose it:** `/api/health` returns `{status, uptime}` and
nothing else. **The gap is one field, on a value the deployment already knows.**

Then the question becomes answerable rather than inferable, and the comparison is against a fact
instead of a branch name. **Note the disclosure question is already live in that file** — the tenant
count was deliberately moved behind authentication there — so *where* the commit is reported is a
decision, not a detail.

> **THE RULE: a programme-state claim that a capability is LIVE shall name the commit the deployment
> serves, or shall say "committed, not deployed". Those are different facts and this programme has
> been writing the first while measuring the second.**

---

## STANDING RULE — A CATCH-ALL 404 IS NOT AN EMPTY COLLECTION

**Recorded 2026-08-06. The distinction is the EXECUTION PLANE'S, measured from its side against the
live Intelligence Plane, and it is recorded here because this plane emits both signals and had not
distinguished them.**

> **A `200` with nothing in it is a SUCCESS. A `404` from an unmounted route is UNAVAILABILITY.**
> Reading the second as the first makes **a plane given an unusable path indistinguishable from a
> plane with nothing to do.**

**IT IS THE ABSENT-`workPath` RULE ONE LAYER DOWN, WHERE IT IS HARDER TO SEE.**
[`work-path-distribution.ts`](../packages/tenant-onboarding-engine/src/engine/work-path-distribution.ts)
already states the outer form: *no route known* is a deployment fact, *route known, empty collection*
is a positive assertion that nothing is pending (R-05.27), and conflating them leaves a tenancy
permanently idle while work accumulates, reporting itself healthy. **The outer form is visible because
the field is absent — there is nothing to misread.** The inner form arrives as a well-formed HTTP
response with a status code, and *"the route answered"* and *"the router had no such route"* look
alike to any caller that branches on `res.ok` alone.

**IT IS ALSO R-05.5 / P-78.2's TAXONOMY AT A THIRD BOUNDARY.** ADR-0078 split *the responder answered*
from *what it answered with can be trusted*. This splits **the responder answered ABOUT THIS ROUTE**
from **the responder answered about no route at all** — and a catch-all `404` is precisely a response
that is well-formed, authoritative, and about nothing.

**THE INSTRUMENT REQUIREMENT THIS IMPLIES, AND THE EP'S MEASUREMENT MET IT.** *"I got a 404"* is not
evidence a route is unmounted unless a **deliberately-absent negative control under the same prefix**
produces the same bytes, and a **known-mounted sibling** produces something different. The EP probed
`/api/packages/{hash}` — which answered in its own vocabulary — and a deliberate absentee, which was
byte-identical, **across real, wrong, garbage and absent credentials**. Without both controls a
catch-all `404` and an authorisation `404` are the same observation.

> **THE RULE FOR THIS PLANE: any route that can return an empty collection SHALL be distinguishable,
> by a caller holding only the response, from the same path when no route is mounted.** A shared
> catch-all that answers for both is a fail-open port one layer out.

---

## STANDING RULE — P-78.4's SECOND RECORD, AND THE TIGHTER FIT MEASURED AT THE UPDATE CHANNEL

**Recorded 2026-08-06, implementing OBL-006 (the update receiver in the generator).** P-78.4 reason 2
rejects a retrieval acknowledgement because *"a second record of one fact is two records that can
disagree."* The update channel is the same shape **one degree worse**, and the degree is the finding:

> **THE QUEUE'S `status` IS ALREADY THE RECORD OF WHETHER AN EVENT WAS APPLIED** — the Intelligence
> Plane reads it, and the Execution Plane's own poll filters on it. **The ack is a SECOND record of
> that same fact, written by the party that never checked.** And when the two disagree the second
> does not merely contend with the first — **IT OVERWRITES IT, BECAUSE ACKING IS WHAT MOVES THE
> FIRST. THE UNRELIABLE RECORD OVERWRITES THE RELIABLE ONE BY CONSTRUCTION.**

**Where P-78.4 forbids a second record because the two could disagree, here they CANNOT disagree for
long enough to be noticed.** A retrieval ack that contradicted the evidence hash would leave two
readable values and a discoverable contradiction. An ack that contradicts the queue leaves **one**
value — the ack's — and the fact it contradicted is gone from the only channel able to report it.

**THE MEASURED INSTANCE, WHICH IS WHY THIS IS NOT A RESTATEMENT.** The generated update agent's
`applySolutionUpdate` **deliberately refuses an unsigned or unhashed platform update** (INV-3,
ADR-0007 §4) — the file's own header names that refusal as its purpose — and the caller then acked
the event unconditionally and logged `applied`. **The strongest safety property in the file was
erased by the only channel that could report it**, in every generated tenant, and no gate reddened:
the refusal was correct, the ack was well-formed, the queue drained, and the Intelligence Plane's
