view was *"the tenancy applied the update it had in fact declined."*

**THE REPAIR IS THE SHAPE, NOT THE BRANCH.** Every handler now RETURNS whether it applied, and the
ack is derived from that return and from nothing else — so the second record can no longer be written
by a party that did not check. **Three classes stay pending and say so:** a deliberate refusal, a
failed write, and **an UNRECOGNISED TYPE**, which exits at the catch-all having matched no branch.
The third is the one no test would have asked for: an Execution Plane generated before an event type
existed was silently consuming events it did not understand, which is indistinguishable at this plane
from having applied them.

> **THE GENERAL RULE: A PARTY MAY ONLY WRITE A RECORD OF A FACT IT CHECKED.** Where the record is
> *also* the mechanism that closes the fact — as an ack is — the requirement is not hygiene, it is
> the only thing standing between a refusal and its erasure.

---

## STANDING RULE — a failure to REACH and a refusal on ARRIVAL are never one signal

> **Any type that represents "could not reach it" and "it said no" with one value has discarded the only fact the caller needs: whether to retry or to stop.**

Defended three times in one session, at three boundaries, each time against a simpler alternative:

| Boundary | Could not reach | Said no | What a collapse would have destroyed |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Stage** (ADR-0071) | `notApplicable` — *there was nothing to review* | `refused` — *I did the work and the answer is no* | a triad that declines versus a triad with no input; a run to fix versus a run to re-scope |
| **Publication** (ADR-0072) | absent from the result | `published: false` with a reason | a suite half-written into a customer's tool versus one never submitted |
| **Transport** (`WriteOutcome<T>`) | a thrown error — the tool was unreachable | `published: false` — the tool refused | **retry versus stop**, in its purest form |
| **Agent** (`AgentOutput<O>`, F1) | *not this agent's question* — no contributions declared | `evidenced: false` with a reason — it looked and the criterion is not met | **who is entitled to conclude.** An agent reports a FINDING; the domain concludes. There is no `refuse` on `AgentOutput` and there will not be one |

**Every collapse would have been simpler, and simplicity is why they keep being proposed.** One boolean, one status, one catch-all error. The cost is invisible at the point of the decision and appears later as an operator retrying something that will never succeed, or abandoning something that would have worked on the next attempt.

### THE RULE ALSO FORBIDS THE OPPOSITE CONVERSION — RULED 2026-08-05, ADR-0076 §6 B2

> **`policy-review` and `guardrail-review` KEEP `notApplicable`. That is a RULING, not an omission, and it is recorded here so that no later reader completes what looks unfinished.**

B2's instruction was *"the canonical triad emits `refuse` rather than `notApplicable`"*. **Applied uniformly it would have been wrong, and measuring the three stages before changing them is the only reason that was caught.** The three are not the same shape:

| Stage | Predicate | Negative means | Outcome | Why |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `policy-review` | `story !== undefined` | **pure absence** | **`notApplicable` — KEPT** | there is no *did-not-approve* state to reach; a refusal would claim a review ran |
| `guardrail-review` | `testDesign !== undefined` | **pure absence** | **`notApplicable` — KEPT** | as above |
| `architecture-review` | `components.length > 0` | **two things at once** | **SPLIT** | absent → `notApplicable`; authored-but-empty → `refuse` |

**A stage that emits `refuse` when nothing arrived is the same defect as one that emits `notApplicable` when it reviewed and objected — the collapse, running the other way.** The rule is symmetric and has always been; every prior instance happened to need widening, so the direction was never tested. **This is the first instance where honouring the rule meant declining to convert**, and it is the reason the entry exists: an implementer reading *"the triad emits `refuse`"* against two stages that still emit `notApplicable` would reasonably read the work as half-done.

**`architecture-review`'s conflation is the finding underneath.** One predicate answered two questions and reported both with *"no automation architecture was authored to review"* — **untrue in the case where one WAS authored and carries no components.** D-013's shape: a message asserting a state the value contradicts. **Reachability decided which half mattered**: the absent branch is unreachable by the runner's own sequencing (D-019), the empty branch is reachable, and **the refusal added is the reachable one**. No refusal predicate was invented; `length > 0` was already the code's condition and only its two causes were routed correctly. What this capability's architecture review *should* refuse on remains `UNDECIDED — Functional Testing` (ADR-0076 §4.4).

### THE PRE-LANDING CHECK, IN ITS CORRECTED FORM — this replaces both the three-boundary framing and the one-directional question that followed it

> **Does this value MATCH what its producer is positioned to know — in either direction?**

**The one-directional version was wrong, and the way it was found is the more useful half.** It read *"does this value say MORE than its producer knows?"*, which catches a verdict that was handed over, acceptances never observed, and attempts counted as outcomes. It does **not** catch the opposite: a value that says **less** than its producer knows, and therefore cannot carry the fact it purports to.

**The instance, found one turn after the question was written, in a field written under it.** `certificationContributions` was `readonly string[]` — bare criterion names. Measured: the thirteen domains declare **109 criterion instances under 35 distinct names**, sixteen of them shared, three declared by all thirteen. An agent declaring `'immutable-output'` would name one of thirteen and identify none. The producer knew perfectly well whose criterion it had evidenced; **the type could not carry it.**

**Too much and too little are the same defect**: a mismatch between the claim and the knowledge. The remedy differs — narrow the claim, or widen the type — but the check is one question, and it must be asked in both directions or it finds only half.

**HOW IT WAS FOUND, because the order was decisive.** By **applying the rule to a field written under it**, one turn after writing it, and by **measuring the criterion namespace rather than by using the field**. Using it would have surfaced the ambiguity only once agents disagreed about what a name meant — after adoption, at some number of agents between one and 144. Measuring the namespace found it at **zero**. **A rule is worth most when turned on the work done under it, and soonest.**

The four-boundary table is the evidence; **this question is the rule**. The first three boundaries separate two kinds of *failure*; the agent boundary separates two kinds of *authority* — what a component **found** from what it is entitled to **conclude**. Those are the same rule seen from two sides: a type that lets a component report a conclusion it cannot reach has discarded the layering, exactly as a type merging reach and refusal discards the remedy.

**THE UNIFICATION IS THE POINT, and it is what turns this register's catalogue into something usable.** Four findings recorded separately, months of work apart, are one question asked of four signatures:

| Finding | The value | What its producer was positioned to know |
|---|---|---|
| **D-012** | `certificationSummary` emitted a **verdict** | `executive-reporting` had been *handed* one — it had computed nothing |
| **D-023 / ruling (b)** | `publishedObjects` counted **acceptances** | the domain knew only what it had **submitted**; the SPI could not report acceptance |
| **D-029 (i)** | the links tally counted **outcomes** | the agent knew only how many calls it had **attempted** |
| **F1 / `AgentOutput`** | an agent reporting `refused` would claim a **run-level conclusion** | an agent knows one finding, not the run |

**Each was found by measurement, after it shipped. All four were visible in the signature before it landed.** `{ published: true }` cannot express acceptance; a `verdict` field on a summary that computes nothing cannot be a verdict; a tally over a literal cannot count outcomes. **That is the difference between a catalogue and a rule: the catalogue tells you what went wrong; the question tells you where to look at a type you have not yet written.**

Collapsing at the agent boundary would have given 144 things the power to stop a run.

**THE TABLE ABOVE IS A PRE-LANDING CHECK, NOT A POST-MORTEM.** It is applied to any SPI change *before* it lands: for each operation, what does the caller see when the target could not be reached, and what does it see when the target refused? If the answer is the same value, the change is not ready. Two of the three instances were found by tracing an existing type; the third by refusing to fold a refusal into a `catch`. **A reviewer holding this rule would have found all three from the signatures alone** — none required running anything, and all three had been in the tree for as long as the SPIs had.

### THE SECOND PRE-LANDING CHECK — a census reports the depth it ranged to (D-121)

> **Does this measurement RANGE OVER the dimension a defect could live in — and does its output say
> how deep it went?**

**A census that cannot reach a dimension is not wrong. It is SILENT, and silence reads as absence.**
That is the difference between this check and §17.1's: **§17.1 asks whether a number MOVES when the
thing it counts moves; this asks whether the instrument can SEE the axis the thing moves along.** A
dimension the instrument never ranged over produces the same output as a dimension that is clean, and
nothing in the result distinguishes them.

**The instance that earned it.** D-121 measured *which required top-level fields are never sent* and
found **seven**. The parser finds **eight**. The eighth is `validity.reusableWhileUnavailable` —
**nested inside a section that IS emitted and is itself incomplete.** A top-level field census
answers *"is this key present?"*, and it returns the same value for a section that is complete and
one that is present-and-partial. **The divergence was one level deeper than the entry that found it,
and the depth was a property of the instrument rather than of the defect.**

**It generalises past field censuses**, which is why it sits here rather than in D-121 alone. Any
measurement over a structured subject has a reach — a symbol scan cannot see a file path (D-077); a
top-level key census cannot see a nested field; a call-site search cannot see an enforcement that
does not call (D-117's correction: `SealedPackageStore.put` enforces three of the contract's fields
without ever calling the parser, so searching for the parser's call sites could not find it).
**Three findings, months apart, one shape: the instrument's reach was never stated, so its silence
was read as a clean result.**

**The obligation, and it is mechanical rather than diligent:** a census, count or audit **states the
depth and the axes it ranged over, in its output**, so a reader can tell *"nothing there"* from
*"never looked there."* An unstated reach is the measurement analogue of `NOT MEASURED` being
counted as a pass (CHARTER §17.1) — and, like that rule, the correct report is the honest narrower
one, never the silent broader-looking one.

### THE PROBE PATTERN — a refusal-shaped result is the answer that looks like success

> **Before reading a refusal as the subject refusing, ask what a DELIBERATELY VALID input returns.
> Where the two are identical, you measured a layer above the subject.**

**This is the second pre-landing check's sibling on the live-path side**, and it is stated separately
because the failure has a specific shape that the census version does not: **the fallthrough is
itself a refusal, so it looks like the property working.**

**Three instances, and the third is the one that shows it is a pattern rather than two accidents:**

| | Probe | What answered | Read alone, it would have said |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | `POST /v1/execute` → **405** | **Azure Blob Storage's error page** — `/v1/*` never reaches the application | *"the route exists and was called with the wrong verb"* |
| **2** | `store.put(gateway envelope)` → `SealedPackageWriteRefused` | the **fallthrough** refusal — `{}` earns the identical message | *"the gateway's shape is wrong in a specific way"* |
| **3** | `POST /v1/evidence` → **six identical 401s**, then **six identical 400s** | the **auth layer**, then the **validation layer** — one and two levels above the subject | *"the ingress refuses everything"* |

**Instance 3 is the sharpest, because both fallthroughs were REFUSALS OF THE RIGHT GENERAL KIND.** The
probe was written to prove that an unbindable evidence reference is refused. It got a refusal. **It
got a refusal to every input, including the ones that must be accepted** — and only the positive
controls separated *"the check works"* from *"nothing reached the check."*

> **A 405, a 401 and a 400 all mean "no", and a property written to produce "no" is confirmed by any
> of them.** That is why this check is not covered by ordinary care: **the wrong answer and the right
> answer are the same word.**

**The obligation:** every probe relied upon as evidence of a refusal lands with **a deliberately valid
sibling that must be ACCEPTED**, and the pair is recorded together. **Where the accepted case is not
constructible, the probe reports what it actually established — that something refused — and not what
it was written to establish.** A second refusing sibling is not a substitute: instance 2's `{}` and
the gateway envelope were both refused, byte-identically, and proved only that neither is a package.

### THE SEVENTH PRE-LANDING CHECK — an additive migration leaves the unmigrated case indistinguishable from a considered exception

> **After an additive migration: what was NOT migrated, and is that recorded as a DECISION — or is it
> residue that now looks like one?**

**Additive migration is the right technique and is not what this check questions.** ADR-0060 §6 M-a
adopted the configuration/secret seam additively so it could land without rewriting working wiring —
correct, and why it succeeded. **The cost is invisible and lands later.**

**The instance (D-129, ADR-0083):** `SESSION_SECRET` moved to the Secret Provider; the **package
signing key** — the platform's highest-value asset — stayed a file, **because it already was one.**
Nothing rejected the Secret Provider for it. **Nothing considered it.**

> **AN AUDITOR LOOKS FOR THE DECISION AND FINDS NONE — AND CANNOT TELL WHICH KIND OF NONE IT IS.**
> A deliberate exception and an untouched case are **the same artefact**: a thing outside the new
> mechanism, with no record either way. So the reader either invents a rationale for it or reports a
> violation, and **both are wrong.** **THE ABSENCE OF THE DECISION IS THE FINDING** — and it is only
> findable by someone who thinks to ask *why is this one different?*, which is not a question any
> gate asks.
>
> **It is D-057's shape at a different altitude.** There, ADR-0072 scoped itself to *"the SPIs
> Section C's publication semantics ran through"* — correct as written, silent about what it
> excluded — and **no artefact recorded that four SPIs were outside it**, so the exclusion was
> invisible to every later reader including the one who wrote D-028. **Same defect: a boundary that
> is correct and unrecorded.**

**The obligation, and it is one line per migration:** a deliberately partial migration **names what
it did not migrate, and whether that is a decision or a deferral.** Not as a caveat — **as the record
that distinguishes the two kinds of none.** A deferral gets an owner; a decision gets a reason;
**residue gets neither, and residue is what this check exists to stop looking like a choice.**

### THE SIXTH PRE-LANDING CHECK — a shared spelling is not an agreement

> **Where two types must agree on a value, is the agreement a PREDICATE — or is it a field name
> spelled the same way in both?**

**A shared spelling is a coincidence that looks like an agreement.** `SignatureEnvelope` called its
key field `signingKeyId` **because the package's `provenance.signingKeyId` is called that** — and the
resemblance was the whole of the guarantee. **Two fields with one name still hold two values, and
nothing compared them.** The names could match perfectly on every run while the values disagreed on
one, and no mechanism anywhere would have noticed.

> **A FIELD NAME IS NOT THE PLACE TO ENCODE AN AGREEMENT THAT CAN BE CHECKED.**

The agreement is now `signatureMatchesProvenance()` — a predicate over the two **values**. And what
it is *not* is stated with it: it proves nothing about the signature's validity, so a caller reading
`true` as *verified* has made D-012's error. It answers one question — *is this signature even
claiming to be about this package's key?* — which is worth answering before spending a verification.

**The convergence was the smaller half of that change.** Unifying two drifted types is tidying; **the
finding is that one of them was carrying a guarantee in its vocabulary.**

### THE COMPILER AS THE DEMONSTRATION, when a type is unified

> **When two shapes are merged, the compile errors are the evidence that they were incompatible —
> and their COUNT AND LOCATION are the measurement.**

Ruling 1 produced **four errors across two conformance suites**, each at a construction site where a
`SignatureEnvelope` literal could not satisfy the contract. **That the type system found every one is
the demonstration that the drift was not stylistic** — a rename would have compiled.

**It is a cheap, honest instrument and it is usually discarded.** The errors are ordinarily treated
as work to get through; **recorded instead, they are the census of exactly where the two
descriptions had diverged**, produced by a mechanism that cannot miss a site.

### REFUSING A RETIRED SHAPE ON A PASSTHROUGH SCHEMA — fail on ABSENCE, never on extras

> **A passthrough schema that refuses by rejecting extra fields refuses nothing.**

Passthrough is required by R-20.4 / C-20.7 — a newer producer must not break an older consumer — so
**unknown fields are ADMITTED BY DESIGN.** A retired shape therefore cannot be excluded by noticing
its extra fields; the only thing that can refuse it is **the absence of the fields the new shape
requires.**

**It is stated because the obvious test is the wrong one.** *"Assert the old shape is rejected"*
passes for the wrong reason if the schema happens to be strict, and **silently passes nothing once
the schema is passthrough** — the retired `SignatureEnvelope` is refused because it lacks `keyId` and
`value`, not because it carries `signature` and `signingKeyId`. **The conformance property asserts
the refusal AND the survival of additive fields together**, because either alone is consistent with
the schema being wrong in the other direction.

### THE FIFTH PRE-LANDING CHECK — the audience for the caveat is not the audience for the verdict

> **Does this qualification travel to the same readers as the thing it qualifies?** Where it does
> not, **the qualification is not a fix** — it is a note attached to something that will be read
> without it.

**A NEGATION STILL CARRIES THE WORD.** The instruction was *"the publication decision SHALL NOT use
the word certified"*. The implementation obeyed it in the obvious way — the emitted reason read
***"ADMISSIBLE IS NOT CERTIFIED — the triad reviews presence, not soundness"*** — which is true, is
the right thing to say, and **is still wrong in that position**.

> A verdict string is **excerpted, logged, quoted into a report, aggregated into a dashboard and
> eventually read without its negation.** The disclaimer travels for one hop; the word travels for
> all of them. **Putting a caveat inside a verdict distributes the claim and localises the
> correction — which is exactly backwards.**

**The distinction now lives in the module's documentation**, which reaches the reader deciding
whether the gate is sufficient, and **the emitted message does not use the word at all**. Same fact,
placed where its audience is.

**IT GENERALISES PAST WORD CHOICE.** Any qualification — a caveat in a log line, a footnote on a
metric, a "provisional" flag beside a score — is subject to it: *who reads the qualified thing, and
do they all get the qualification?* This register already counts the failure at other altitudes:
D-043's reason string carried the number that refuted its own verdict **in the same sentence** and
the verdict was still published; D-012's summary carried a verdict it had been handed. **The
common shape is a true statement placed where its truth does not survive transmission.**

**And it was found by a PROPERTY, not by review** — the conformance test asserts the word's absence
in any field or message, so the caveat failed at authoring time rather than being noticed later in a
dashboard.

### THE THIRD PRE-LANDING CHECK — a WIDENED check states what it stopped seeing (D-126)

> **When a check is loosened to stop false positives, WHICH TRUE POSITIVE DID THE LOOSENING TRADE
> AWAY — and where is that written down?**

**A control narrowed to stop crying wolf is almost always the right change, and it is never a free
one.** Widening the input a check accepts, or the corpus it searches, buys quiet by making some
previously-distinguishable states indistinguishable. **The widening is recorded in the diff. The
detection it surrendered is recorded nowhere**, so the next reader inherits a control that looks
strictly better than the one it replaced.

**The instance that earned it — and it is the second pre-landing check's sibling, one level out.**
`verify-change-control-completeness`'s connectivity property originally read a **hand-listed subset**
of programme files, omitted `DECISIONS.md` — *the actual ADR index* — and reported five connected
ADRs as orphans. It was widened to a **wholesale `program/` scan**, and its own header records why:
*"a check that cries wolf trains reviewers to ignore it, so the corpus is derived, not curated."*
**That reasoning is correct and the widening stands.**

**What it stopped seeing was never written down:** after the widening, *mentioned once anywhere in
programme state* became **indistinguishable from** *named by the index*. **Eight ADRs — including
ADR-0080, accepted the same day and the subject of an open ruling — sat unindexed while three gates
ran over the ADR estate and none ran over the index.** Each ADR was "connected" because some state
file mentioned it.

> **AND THE DIRECTION IS WHAT MADE IT SURVIVE. D-107 found a STALE ROW — a wrong value, which at
> least exists to be compared. This is an ABSENT ROW, and a missing row cannot be stale, cannot be
> diffed, and produces no output for any reader to doubt.** CHARTER §17.1.1 states the mechanism
> exactly: every property over the estate is satisfied **more easily** when a row is missing.

**The obligation:** a change that widens a check's corpus, input domain or tolerance **records the
distinction it just collapsed**, in the check's own source, beside the reason for the widening. **Not
as a caveat — as the specification of the sibling property that is now owed.** The wholesale scan
stays; *is this ADR named by the index* is added beside it, because those were one question before
the widening and are two after it.

### THE FOURTH PRE-LANDING CHECK — one question, one enumeration (ADR-0082 P-82.8)

> **Does this governance question have exactly ONE place that answers it — and is that place an
> ENUMERATION, or is it "however many artefacts someone happened to write"?**

**A question answered by the existence of artefacts has no answer.** When *"which X does rule R
govern?"* is answered by counting the gates, modules or documents that happen to mention X, the
answer changes silently whenever someone writes one or forgets to. **There is no state in which the
answer is wrong**, because there is no place holding it — which is why nothing can be red.

**Caught in drafting rather than after, which is the only reason it is cheap.** ADR-0082 needed a
document-06 gate for the platform's **second** customer-data store.
`verify-data-sovereignty-store.js` is scoped to **one subject by construction** — it hard-codes the
sealed package store's path and **fails closed when that file is absent**, which is CHARTER §17.1.1
applied correctly at authoring time. The cheaper change was a **sibling gate**. It was refused:

> ***"Which stores does document 06 govern?"* must have ONE answer, read from ONE enumeration.** A
> sibling gate makes the answer *"however many gates someone happened to write"* — so the gate takes
> a **subject list**, every property runs per subject, and **an empty list fails closed** exactly as
> the single hard-coded subject does today.

**It is D-126's shape, one step earlier in its life.** There, three gates ran over the ADR estate and
**none over the index**, because no single enumeration owned *"which ADRs exist?"* — and eight ADRs
including ADR-0080 sat unindexed with nothing able to report it. **D-126 is this check failing;
P-82.8 is it passing.** The difference cost one sentence in an ADR, and D-126 cost a gate.

**The obligation:** when a rule acquires its **second** subject, the second subject does not get its
own artefact — **the first artefact acquires an enumeration**, and the enumeration is the answer to
the rule's question. **A third subject then joins a list rather than justifying a third artefact**,
and the empty list fails closed.

### ONE UNBUILT MECHANISM, COUNTED TWICE IN THE PLAN — per-agent behavioural attribution

**Recorded before either consumer is built, because the plan currently pays for it twice.**

| Consumer | What it needs | Currently scoped as |
|---|---|---|
| **The naming gate's closure** (F1) | compare an agent's declared `outputs` and `toolContracts` against what its `handle` actually returns and calls | a caution on the naming gate, unbuilt |
| **The criteria NEGATIVE group** (~24, the criteria step) | attribute an SPI call to the DOMAIN that made it, so `no-execution` can be evidenced by observing that none was made | *"the real cost, and the only part requiring a new mechanism"* |

**They are the same thing: observe an agent running, record what it returned and what it called, compare against what it declared.** The negative group needs the calls; the naming gate needs the calls *and* the returns. **One mechanism, two consumers — estimate it once.**

**D-035 is its justification, and it is unusually concrete.** Fifteen agents declared `stage: 'guardrail-review'` with names like `test.positive`, and their bodies are generators (`purpose: "Generate the happy-path scenario"`, `outputs: Scenario[]`). **A naming gate passes all fifteen** — id prefix resolves, `domain` matches, no duplicates — because it compares a declaration against a list of declarations. The defect was found by a human reading `purpose` and `outputs`, and nothing in the platform could have found it.

> **ESTIMATED 2026-08-04, AND THE CLAIM ABOVE IS WITHDRAWN. It is half tractable, and the tractable half WOULD NOT HAVE CAUGHT D-035 — its own justification.**

**What exists already.** `invocationRecorder` (`capability-framework/src/reasoning.ts:127`) wraps the agent context and records `agent.<id>.invoked`, so **which agents ran is already observable** — identity, not behaviour. And `design-sync-conformance.test.ts` already contains the call-observation mechanism: a `spy<T>()` `Proxy` that records `${spi}.${method}` on every adapter call. **The mechanism exists as test scaffolding and would be promoted, not invented.**

**Where the seam is.** Agents reach adapters through their **input**, not their context — `input.adapter`, `input.adapters`, and only three of seven agent files use one at all. So calls are recorded by wrapping the adapter **at the site that constructs the agent's input**, which is the orchestrator today and the domain under F1. That site knows which agent it is building for, so attribution is free.

**The two comparisons are not equally tractable, and that is the finding:**

| Compare | Tractable | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `toolContracts` **vs observed calls** | **YES** | both are SPI names. `['TestDesignSyncAdapter']` against a recorded `TestDesignSyncAdapter.createTestCase` is a set comparison. Mechanism exists; cost is promoting the `spy` Proxy and threading it through input construction |
| `outputs` **vs observed return** | **NO, as stated** | `outputs` is PROSE — `['Scenario[]']`, `['coverage']`, `['link census']`. There is no convention mapping a prose label to a runtime shape, and inventing one would be a declaration about a declaration |

**THE DECISIVE RESULT: it would not have caught D-035.** Those fifteen agents declare `toolContracts: []` and call nothing — **the tractable comparison passes them.** Their defect is that `purpose` says *"Generate the happy-path scenario"* while `stage` says `guardrail-review`, and **`purpose` is prose, `stage` is a declaration, and the mismatch is between two declarations plus a human's reading of a body.** Neither half of this mechanism touches it.

**THE D-007 CLAIM IS WITHDRAWN, AND IT WAS HELD ON BOTH SIDES.** *"The first time anything in this platform compares behaviour to declaration"* was written, repeated and ruled on before anyone compared it against the declarations it would actually read. **It was made from the SHAPE of the idea** — observe an agent, compare to its declaration — which is compelling and true of the shape and false of the specific fields. **Caught by the estimate, in the same order that caught D-038 and the criterion-namespace collision: measure the thing before designing over it.** Three corrections this section, all from measuring before building, none from review.

**WHAT THE MECHANISM DOES COVER — build it for this:**

- **`toolContracts` drift.** An agent declaring `['TestDesignSyncAdapter']` and calling nothing, or calling an SPI it never declared, is detectable and is a real class — the platform has six recorded D-007 instances and adapter usage is where a declaration most easily rots.
- **The call record the criteria NEGATIVE group needs.** `no-execution`, `no-alm-synchronisation` and the other call-shaped absences are evidenced by observing that no such call was made, attributed to a domain. **That is the same recording, and the reason the two consumers are one mechanism.**

**WHAT IT DOES NOT COVER, and this is not pending:**

> **The naming gate's caution stands UNCLOSED. Nothing planned compares an agent's behaviour to its name, and a human reading is currently the only thing that can.**

**D-035 is the standing evidence and it is not a hypothetical**: fifteen agents named `test.positive`, `test.security` and so on, declaring `stage: 'guardrail-review'`, whose bodies generate scenarios. `toolContracts: []` — accurate. Calls made — none, accurate. **Every mechanical check passes.** The defect lives between `purpose` (prose), `outputs` (prose) and `stage` (a declaration), and it was found by a person reading the body. **Recording it as UNCLOSED rather than PENDING is the point: pending implies something scheduled will close it, and nothing scheduled will.**

**That is a larger claim than either consumer needs, which is why it should be estimated on its own terms rather than as a line item under a gate.** D-007 has six recorded instances and every one was found by reading; the register's standing position is that the suite verifies artefacts and cannot verify the reasoning that selects which artefacts to compare (D-011). **A mechanism that observes an agent and compares it to its own declaration is not a general answer to that — but it is the first partial one, over the 144 declarations most likely to drift.**

### THE SECOND DESIGN-TIME CHECK — a fix inherits its defect's shape

> **If this mechanism ships, what flows through it on the first real run?**

Asked of a thing not yet built, and it sits beside the fourth-boundary question rather than in the register, because **it is a check to apply, not an incident to remember.**

**A fix inherits its defect's shape because it is built in the same vocabulary.** D-015 is *declarations nothing evidences*, so its fix is a declaration — and a declaration with no evidencer is D-015 again. R-13.7 is *properties that cannot fail*, so its evidence is a test — and a test with nothing to observe cannot fail. **The remedy is expressed in the same terms as the disease, which is why it reproduces it: the author reaches for the mechanism the defect made salient.**

**Two instances, both caught before code, both by this question:**

| The fix | What would have flowed through it |
|---|---|
| ADR-0072's negative-path test | **nothing** — no adapter refuses, so it would assert that a failing fixture returns what it was told. C-3's tautology in a new file |
| The 109-criteria mechanism, part 1 | **nothing** — 144 agents declaring `certificationContributions: []`. D-033's shape inside the mechanism built to close D-015 |

**Both answered *nothing* and both stopped there.** That is the check working: it is cheap, it is askable of a design, and it does not require the mechanism to exist.

## THE REFERENCE PATTERN — `design-sync.validation`, recorded explicitly

**It is the only agent in the set written for a world where writes fail**, and it is now correct three times over, for three different reasons:

1. **The missing-id check.** It opens with `if (!input.outcome.testCaseId)` and reports `test-case-exists: false` rather than proceeding. That is D-034's guard, already present — the three agents that lack it are the finding, not this one.
2. **The read-back.** It calls `readTestCase` and checks what the tool actually holds, with its own comment stating why: *"OBSERVED, not assumed. A write that returned successfully and stored nothing is exactly the failure a validation reading its own return value cannot see."* **That survives a lying adapter; a return code does not.**
3. **The port model.** Every check it performs compares what PERSISTED against what was REQUESTED, so it depends on no field the canonical sequence cannot supply — which is why it is the one agent the D-031/D-032 shape mismatch does not touch.

**Section F inherits it as the model for the nine**, and its `readTestCase` is the read-back precedent for ADR-0073 ruling (b).

### THE AGENT-LEVEL FORM OF THE STANDING RULE

> **An agent that writes should be able to say what happened when the write did not.**

The standing rule above governs TYPES — never collapse *could not reach* with *said no*. This is its consequence one level down, for the code that calls them, and it is **Section F's acceptance bar for the agent layer**: an agent is not ported until it can report a failed write, and a report that says only what succeeded is not one.

**The count, as of 2026-08-04:**

| | Then | Now |
|---|---|---|
| Could not report a failed write at all | **four of nine** — traceability, attachments, suite assignment, shared assets | **zero**, since ADR-0073 and `WriteOutcome<T>` |
| Could not check whether their PREDECESSOR succeeded | **three of nine** — traceability, attachments, suite assignment | **zero**, since D-034's fix: each returns `skipped: true` rather than writing against a null id |
| Written for a world where writes fail from the start | **one** — `validation` | one |

**Both halves were latent, not live**, and both would have been ported intact.

### THE LAW, not the observation

> **Neither half was a coding error. Each was CORRECT CODE resting on a guarantee that stopped holding when the layer beneath it became honest.**

**Confirmed at three layers now**, each found by looking downstream of a repair rather than at anything that appeared broken:

| Layer | The guarantee that stopped holding | What had been resting on it |
|---|---|---|
| **Domain** | `publishTests` could only signal partial acceptance by returning a SHORT array | `synchronisation`'s `acknowledged < submitted` branch — died the moment the SPI became honest, and 1,427 tests passed over the silence |
| **Agent · write** | four SPI operations returned type-level literals | four `failureHandling` declarations that were *unimplementable when written*, and the link tally that counted attempts |
| **Agent · sequence** | a create always succeeded, so a null `testCaseId` could not occur | three agents writing links, attachments and a suite assignment against a case that was never created |

**The practical consequence is that "correct" is time-bounded.** Code is correct *against the guarantees available when it was written*, and making a lower layer honest is precisely the act that withdraws one. So the review question is not *is this right?* but **what does this assume that is true only because something below it cannot yet fail?**

**THE THIRD ROW OF THE COUNT ABOVE IS THE FINDING.** Only `validation` was **designed** for failure; the other eight were **retrofitted** for it, in this section, by this work. A retrofitted agent is correct today and rests on exactly the kind of guarantee that has now stopped holding twice. **Section F's acceptance bar therefore carries the question as well as the rule**: of every agent it builds or ports, ask what it assumes that is true only because something below it cannot yet fail — and record the answer, so the next honest layer has somewhere to be checked against.

## A TRUTHFUL NEGATIVE WITH NOWHERE TO GO — the pattern behind D-012, D-019 and D-023

Three entries, three layers, one shape. **Each is a place where the system could not express a negative it had genuinely reached**, and in each the field that appears to carry the negative was doing something else entirely.

| Layer | What it could say | What it could not say | The field that looked like it carried the negative |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Stage** (D-019) | *I did work* · *I did no work* | **the answer is no** | `applicable: false` — which means *nothing to review*, not *review failed* |
| **Domain** (D-012) | `certified: true` | **anything else** — 13/13 hardcoded, `failure: null` throughout | `certified` — a literal, read by nobody |
| **Tool boundary** (D-023) | *published* | **failed** — `{ published: true }` is a type-level literal | `PublishedObject.status` — derivable only as `'published'` |

**In each case the workaround was to misreport a different fact.** A stage wanting to decline had to claim it had done no work. A domain wanting to report a gap had nowhere to put it, so the run reported success. A publication that failed had to be reported as a publication that succeeded. **The system's honesty was bounded by its vocabulary, and the vocabulary was assumed adequate because nothing had ever needed the missing word.**

**THE DETECTION MECHANISM IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING: each stayed invisible until something DOWNSTREAM became accurate enough to disagree.**

- D-012 was invisible while the reported verdict was the only verdict. **FT-037 gave `executive-reporting` its own computed verdict, and the echo became visible by contrast** — two values that should have matched, and did not.
- D-021 was invisible while both certification mechanisms were unconditionally positive. **The same computed verdict made one of them capable of saying NOT CERTIFIED, and the disagreement surfaced immediately.**
- D-023 was invisible while `publicationStatus` was never interrogated. **Ruling that zero-published must become a negative finding forced the question *what does status actually reflect*, and the answer was: whatever the SPI permits, which is one value.**
- D-019 was invisible while nothing asked a triad stage to refuse. **Designing the port of 20 triad agents forced the question, and the framework had no answer.**

**Accuracy is contagious in exactly one direction.** Making one component honest does not fix its neighbours — it makes their dishonesty *visible*, because an accurate value and an inaccurate one that used to agree now disagree, and a disagreement is a signal where a shared silence was not. **That is why these three were found in the order they were found, and it is the strongest available argument for making a component accurate even when nothing yet consumes the accuracy.** The consumer that matters is not the code downstream; it is the next contradiction.

**THE OPERATIONAL COROLLARY — where to look for the next instance. STATED IN ITS BOUNDED FORM, which replaces the unbounded version it was first written in.**

> **Look DOWNSTREAM OF WHATEVER WAS JUST MADE HONEST, not at what appears broken — but new honesty exposes silence ONLY WHERE A CONSUMER HAD ALREADY BUILT ON THE OLD BEHAVIOUR. A capability added where nothing yet consumes it disturbs nothing, and its correctness must be established by ASSERTION rather than by contradiction.**

What appears broken is a poor search space: every instance looked fine, and had looked fine for as long as it existed. What is *downstream of a new accuracy* is a good search space **when something is standing on it**, because that is where a value that used to agree with its neighbour has just stopped agreeing. The search is for a **disagreement**, and a disagreement is mechanically detectable where a defect is a judgement.

**The bound was measured, not reasoned** — see the prediction scoreboard below. The first production `refuse` added a new control-flow path through the platform's certification machinery, and **not one of 1,429 tests noticed**, because nothing consumed refusal. The corollary made no prediction there and should not have.

**THE OPERATIONAL CONSEQUENCE.** Where there is a consumer, contradiction does the work and the corollary tells you where to look. **Where there is no consumer, assertion is the only route to correctness** — which is why the first `refuse` shipped with four assertions and a negative control rather than with a green suite, and why a green suite over a new capability is evidence of absence rather than absence of a problem.

**CROSS-REFERENCE, because the same fact carries opposite implications and both hold.** `continuousLearningAgents` were resolved PORTED with no canonical consumer (`RETIREMENT_RESOLUTION_REGISTER.md` §7), on the argument that *"no consumer today"* is what drops a capability the platform later needs. That argument is untouched by this bound. **"No consumer" is not grounds to drop, AND is grounds to require assertion** — the first is about whether the capability should exist, the second about how its correctness can be known. Confusing them would either delete needed capability or ship unverified capability, and the register has instances pointing at both.

**CONFIRMED, not merely illustrated — see D-024.** Landing ADR-0072 made the publication SPI honest, and the very next thing downstream of it — the first consumer whose code was touched — turned out to carry a declaration that had been false since it was written. It was not found by looking for defects in dev-change. It was found by making the layer beneath it capable of telling the truth and then reading what sat on top. **The corollary predicted the location, and the location produced an instance on the first application.**

**THE COROLLARY'S SECOND HALF, and it points the opposite way from the first.**

> **Making a layer honest can DESTROY a downstream heuristic that depended on the dishonesty — inferring refusal from a short array only worked while refusal was inexpressible.**

So the search has two objects, not one: **look downstream of new honesty for newly-visible silence, AND for inferences that silence was holding up.** The first half finds things that were always wrong and are now visible. The second finds things that were *right* until the repair and are wrong after it.

**THE CONFIRMING INSTANCE IS A REGRESSION IN THIS PROGRAMME'S OWN WORK, AND IT IS THE HEADLINE OF SECTION C's OPENING.** **ADR-0072 killed Section B's synchronisation failure branch, and 1,427 tests passed over the silence** — in the section whose entire subject was making domains able to report failure. The branch read `publishedTestIds.length < submitted`, which worked only because the SPI returned a `string[]` that could signal partial acceptance by being **short**. One outcome per test makes the array always full length, so the condition became unreachable the moment the SPI became honest. Measured: variant F stopped producing a synchronisation finding. Repaired by counting refused outcomes, and the finding is now strictly better — the connector says *which* case it refused and *why*, where the domain could previously only count.

**Nothing detected it, and the reason is recorded separately as D-026:** the negative-path variant that exists to prove these branches fire asserts nothing about *which* findings it produces.

**THE COROLLARY TESTED AS A PREDICTOR, 2026-08-04 — and it found its own limit.** Before implementing the first production `refuse`, six falsifiable predictions were written down and then measured. **All six held.** The instructive one is P4.

| # | Prediction, written BEFORE the run | Measured |
|---|---|---|
| P1 | `certify()` returns `certified: false` with `firstRefusal` populated, for the first time in production | **held** — `release-certified`, `refused: synchronisation reported a publication failure: …` |
| P2 | `runtime-entry-point-bridge.ts:103` throws; that branch has never executed in production | **held** — threw, and the message had never been read by anyone before |
| P3 | `progressedTo()`'s gate ordering stays unexercised, because the refusal lands on the last gate | **held** — the silence stayed silent |
| P4 | **Nothing in the existing suite breaks** | **held** — 1,429 → 1,432, all additions, 0 failures |
| P5 | `canonical-runner-equivalence` does not break | **held** — it uses clean connectors |
| P6 | Both mechanisms report negatively for variant R, agreeing by shared cause; D-021 untouched | **held** — asserted in a test so the agreement is not later read as reconciliation |

**P4 IS THE LIMIT, AND IT IS WORTH MORE THAN THE FIVE CONFIRMATIONS.** A new control-flow path was added through the platform's certification machinery — a stage refusing, `certify()` taking a branch it had never taken, an entry point throwing — and **not one of 1,429 existing tests noticed.** The corollary did not predict a discovery here, and the reason is precise: **there was no downstream inference to disturb, because nothing consumed refusal — refusal did not exist.**

So the corollary's scope is narrower than "new honesty exposes neighbouring silence". It is:

> **New honesty exposes silence only where a consumer had already built on the old behaviour.** A capability added where nothing yet consumes it disturbs nothing, and its correctness must be established by assertion rather than by contradiction.

That is the same shape as the argument about continuous-learning's missing consumer, arriving from the opposite direction — and it says when the corollary is a search strategy and when it is not. **Where nothing consumes the new capability, the only available evidence is a test someone deliberately writes**, which is why the first `refuse` shipped with four assertions and a negative control rather than with a green suite.

**The warning half.** Each repair exposes the next layer's silence, so the work does not converge from the top — it walks. Section C's first production `refuse` is the next one already scheduled: it tests ADR-0071's primitive by using it, and if the primitive is wrong, the way it will be found is that something disagrees.

**A GOOD OUTCOME, RECORDED AS ONE — a sovereignty invariant held by a conformance assertion, and it held.** Implementing Section C ruling (b), the publication path was changed to take each object's external reference from the connector's outcome. Applied uniformly that is correct for four of the five kinds and **wrong for evidence**: it replaces an Execution-Plane custody locator with whatever identity the customer's test tool assigned, and **evidence crosses by reference — the locator IS the reference (INV-1)**. `synchronisation-conformance` asserts that every published evidence reference starts with `execution-plane://`, and it failed. Evidence now keeps its locator and takes only its *status* from the outcome.

**Recorded because this register is mostly a catalogue of what nothing was watching, and here something was.** The assertion was written by someone who thought to pin the *prefix* rather than the *shape* — a sovereignty property, expressed narrowly enough to fail. Nothing else in the change would have caught it: the types were satisfied, all other tests passed, and the resulting run would have looked correct while quietly moving evidence identity out of Execution-Plane custody. **Evidence-by-reference survived a change that would have broken it silently, because someone asserted the prefix.**

**R-13.7 APPLIED TO ITSELF — why ADR-0072 ships without its negative-path test, and why writing one would have been the violation.**

ADR-0072's third enforcement mechanism is a conformance test asserting that a refused publication is visible to its caller. It is **sequenced, not written**, and the reasoning is recorded because the decision alone would read as a gap someone forgot to close.

**No production code path acts on `published: false`.** Every failure branch introduced by ADR-0072 is a marked, unreachable fallback (its §4 work list), because deciding what a failed publication *means* is owned per capability. A test written today could therefore only construct a deliberately-failing adapter and assert that the adapter returns what it was just told to return. **That assertion cannot fail** — it tests the test fixture. It is C-3's tautology (D-015) in a new file, with a green tick beside it.

**So the rule forbids the artefact that would demonstrate compliance with the rule.** Writing an unfalsifiable test in order to satisfy a rule against unfalsifiable properties is the same self-refutation as forcing a green out of C-3 to satisfy a rule about honest reds — and R-13.7's threshold was deliberately set at *"before its pass is relied upon"* rather than *"before it is merged"* for exactly this case: **a property may land absent and honest, and must not land present and vacuous.**

**The test lands with its first real consumer**, which is Section C's first production `refuse` — where there is behaviour to observe and the assertion can fail. Recorded so that the interval is a stated deferral rather than an omission, and so the next reader does not close the gap by writing the tautology.

**R-13.7 clause 2 — FIRST PRE-EMPTIVE CATCH, 2026-08-04, hours after adoption.** Building ADR-0071's conformance suite, the test for *a refusal requires a stated reason* was written as `assert.throws(() => runCapability(...), /requires a stated reason/)`. **It cannot fire.** `runCapability` catches `StageError` and converts it to `failedAt`/`failure`, so the assertion could only pass if the framework were broken in an unrelated way — a probe aimed at the wrong mechanism, reporting on a branch that never executed. It was rewritten to assert the runner's failure record and the `stage.failed` audit event.

**This is the third occurrence of that shape and the first caught before it shipped.** The first was the C-5 stale-citation probe, which tripped `UNDECLARED` and `FALSE CITATION` while claiming to prove `STALE` — found by reading which violation it actually produced, after the fact. The second was the triad grep whose eight-item output was hardcoded into a probe that then reported four stages as unimplemented — found by reading the source before writing the conclusion down, after the fact. **This one was caught by running the probe and seeing it fail**, which is clause 2's mechanism operating as designed rather than a reviewer noticing.

Worth stating precisely, because the distinction is the whole value of the clause: **the first two were caught by care, and care had already failed twice in the same session.** The third was caught by an obligation that does not depend on the author being alert at the moment it matters. That is the difference between a practice and a rule, and it is the argument §18 makes for R-13.7 arriving as evidence rather than as assertion.

**D-015's second half is the more general finding: the vacuous-green class RECURRED in a property written AFTER the first instance was fixed, in the same section, by the same author, four days later.**

| | C-5, first instance | C-3, second instance |
|---|---|---|
| The bug | `Object.values()` over a `Map` returns `[]`, so the supplied-value set was empty and nothing could match | `observed: criteriaDeclared > 0`, where `criteriaDeclared` was `E.CANONICAL_DOMAIN_CRITERIA_COUNT ?? 115` — **that export exists nowhere in the tree**, so it always resolved to the literal, and `115 > 0` is a tautology |
| What it looked like | A green property on a known defect | A green property on a 109-to-0 gap |
| Why it was green | Because it was broken | Because it could not fail |
| How it was found | By hand, reading the code | By hand, reading the code |
| What would have found it | Nothing in the suite. Both were, and are, invisible to all 69 gates |

**The recurrence is the point, and it should not be read as a lapse.** A property is written by an author who has just convinced themselves the thing is true; the natural expression of that conviction is an assertion that holds, and a tautology holds hardest. Green is the outcome the author expects, so it is the outcome least likely to be interrogated — **a property that cannot fail is indistinguishable, from the outside and in every report, from one that passes.** That is the whole argument for *prove it can fail before its pass means anything*: not diligence, which demonstrably did not survive four days and one file, but a mechanical obligation attached to writing a property at all. It now has two instances in one section, which is the strongest available evidence that it is a default rather than an accident.

**PROMOTED — CHARTER §18 R-13.7, adopted 2026-08-04.** The obligation was a working instruction appearing nowhere in `CHARTER.md`, which under §6 is precisely the *declared-without-enforcement* shape the charter exists to prevent: the rule governing how properties are written was itself governed by nothing. It now has two clauses — a property must be shown to FAIL before its pass is relied upon, and the demonstration must fault the **source of truth** and show the **specific branch under test** executing. Mechanism 1 is in force; mechanisms 2 and 3 are sequenced in `PROJECT_STATE.md` §9.5 with the D-009 circularity named inside the rule rather than legislated around. **Clause 2 was added on evidence that arrived after the first draft** — see D-018.

**D-011 — THE INSTRUMENT IS NOW VALID. Section E, 2026-08-04. It reported `orphanCount: 0` TWICE, for two unrelated reasons, and neither zero meant what a reader would take it to mean.**

| | Cause | Surviving closure | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Degeneracy 1** | `authoring-bridge.mjs:37` imports the package BARREL, and `export … from` was counted as an import edge, so the surviving closure equalled the whole package | **52 of 52** | A named import resolves through re-export chains to the module that DEFINES the name; only namespace, default, side-effect and dynamic imports take a module whole |
| **Degeneracy 2** | With the barrel fixed the count was *still* zero: **all three retirement targets were inside the surviving closure.** `RETIRING \ SURVIVING` cannot find what is reachable only through the retiring set while `SURVIVING ⊇ RETIRING` | **41** | Traversal is CUT at the retirement targets, measuring *"what is orphaned AFTER re-pointing"* — the question ADR-0061 §6 step 6 actually leaves open. The cut edges are published as the re-pointing work list, not discarded |
| **Valid** | — | **31** | `orphanCount: 10`, `unresolvedNames: 0`, `VALIDITY: measured` |

**The second degeneracy is the more instructive, and it was only visible because a zero was disbelieved twice.** The first fix produced a *different* wrong answer with the same value, and had the tool been trusted at that point the conclusion — *"retirement orphans nothing"* — would have been identical, differently wrong, and backed by a demonstrably repaired instrument. **A set difference returns an arithmetically correct answer to whatever question its inputs encode and says nothing about whether that was the question.** This is D-007's axis pointed at a measurement rather than a declaration, and it is the same failure as the C-5 fault probe that fired the wrong branch — recorded together because they are one class.

**MEASURED RESULT — 10 orphans, 9 exporting constructible units, and it corrects ADR-0069 P-69.2's two named instances in both directions:**

> **SUPERSEDED 2026-08-05 — THE RESULT BELOW IS 10 AND THE ANSWER IS 9. SEE D-074.** This block was measured against `eca53fb`; `4d79e59` then composed design synchronisation onto the canonical runtime and moved **`agents/design-sync.ts` from orphan to load-bearing** (`domains/synchronisation.ts:152`). The fourth bullet below — *"`agents/design-sync.ts` IS"* — was true when written and is now false. **It is left standing rather than edited**, because the shape of the error is the evidence: an instrument recorded as *valid* on a tree that changed the next day, and a validity claim that carried no tree state. The current count is **9 orphans / 6 944 lines**, and it is authoritative in `retirement-inventory.json`, not here.

- **7 agent-collection modules** — `agents/authoring.ts`, `agents/automation-execution-healing.ts`, `agents/continuous-learning.ts`, `agents/design-sync.ts`, `agents/repository-and-authoring.ts`, `agents/review-board.ts`, `agents/story-and-test.ts`, exporting the twelve agent collections that constitute all 144 agents. **This is the capability loss, and it is what retirement actually removes.**
- **3 retirement targets themselves** — `capability.ts`, `orchestrators.ts`, `catalogue.ts`.
- **`observation-interpretation.ts` is NOT an orphan.** P-69.2 named it as an unported capability; `authoring-bridge.mjs:40` imports `decomposeAcceptanceCriteria` and `classifyArtefact` from it **directly**, so it survives re-pointing untouched. It remains outside `CANONICAL_DOMAIN_SEQUENCE`, which is a separate and still-open question — *not in the canonical sequence* and *deleted by retirement* are different problems, and P-69.2 conflated them.
- **`design-sync-adapters.ts` is NOT an orphan; `agents/design-sync.ts` IS.** P-69.2 named "the Phase 6.9 design-synchronisation adapter" — the **adapter survives** (it is in the surviving closure) and the **agents** are orphaned. The named instance was the wrong half.

**FOUR RE-POINTING OBLIGATIONS, each verified by hand against source rather than taken from the tool:** `authoring-bridge.mjs` → `capability.ts` (`createFunctionalTestingEngine`), → `catalogue.ts` (`buildCatalogue`), → `orchestrators.ts` (`FunctionalTestingOrchestrator`); and `registry/reasoning-publication.ts:36` → `capability.ts` (`import type { EngineState }`, defined at `capability.ts:255`). The fourth is not in ADR-0061's re-pointing scope, which names only the bridge — a type-only import, so it costs nothing at runtime, but it is a surviving module depending on a retirement target and the ADR does not mention it.

**Retirement remains blocked, now for a measured reason rather than an unmeasurable one.** P-69.2's precondition is that every inventory entry is resolved PORTED or DELIBERATELY DROPPED with a stated reason; ten entries are now enumerated and none is resolved. The tool is a candidate for `run-all.js` as a standing gate — the class recurs at every deletion — but it should not be registered while `verify-functional-completeness` is red, because under D-009 a red gate cannot record a fault proof.

**D-005 resolved 2026-07-23 (verification reconciliation).** The whole tree was brought to green without inflating any claim. Six red gates were fixed at root cause: three rule-citations naming the unversioned tool bootstrap file as their source were repointed to the versioned CHARTER (vendor-neutrality); two test files gained TRACEABILITY blocks and a mis-cited criterion `C-11.16` (actually Rule R-11.16) was corrected to `C-11.5` (implementation-traceability); ADR-0026 §8 was rewritten into resolvable paths (change-control); the five standalone capability gates (`verify-devchange-certification`, `verify-performance-conformance`, `verify-sectest-conformance`, `verify-pentest-conformance`, `verify-pentest-completeness`) were registered in `run-all.js`, each given a recorded, replayed fault proof (two CREATE-mode "26th-document" probes and two `patch`-mode inert-orchestrator/dormant-agent probes — a new, more robust fault mode added to the recorder), and the closure baseline was deliberately re-cut to admit ADR-0023…ADR-0029 and 25 gates. The full suite is **25/25 green with 27 fault proofs**. What still remains of the original D-005 concern — *the tree is uncommitted* — is now the only residue: the git history split into per-capability commits is the final step, and until it lands the work is green but not yet under version control.

**D-010 update (2026-08-04) — the probe was one line of a cluster, and removing it exposed the rest.** The `devBootstrap.mjs` health poll is **removed**; readiness is now expressed solely by `docker-compose.dev.yml`, which already declared `depends_on: execution-plane-dev: condition: service_healthy` against the EP's own `healthcheck`. The poll duplicated a runtime guarantee that is *stronger* than it was — the dependency holds before the process exists — so nothing was traded away. `node --check` passes.

**A second, deeper instance was then found, and it had been invisible to the gate.** `launcher/services/executionPlaneService.mjs:6` defaults a parameter to `fetch` (`{ fetchImpl = fetch }`) and calls it as `fetchImpl(healthUrl)` at line 9 — a second Intelligence-Plane health probe against the Execution Plane, on the *launcher's* path rather than a dev script's. A call-shaped matcher cannot see it, so the egress gate was extended to detect `fetch` captured as a **value**, not only invoked. Aliasing the client under another name is the obvious way past a call matcher, and this was not hypothetical: it was already in the tree, hiding a real violation. The gate now names it.

**The cluster, and the single decision underneath it.** Nine Intelligence-Plane references hold, validate, thread or consume the Execution Plane's address: `bootstrapContext.mjs:8` (the field), `configurationService.mjs:5` (reads the env var), `configurationValidator.mjs:8` (requires it), `executionPlaneValidator.mjs:7,13` (requires a valid https URL), `bindingsService.mjs:26` (threads it), `devBootstrap.mjs:19,21,40` (reads and passes it), `generateBindings.mjs:29,34,37` (emits `send: (pkg, signature, attempt) => epSend(executionPlaneEndpoint, …)`), plus `docker-compose.dev.yml:30` which supplies it. **Eight of the nine exist only to serve the ninth.** `generateBindings` emits an IP that **pushes** the sealed package to the EP — while [ADR-0037](../docs/adr/ADR-0037-execution-target-simplification.md) §116 declares the opposite: the Execution Plane *"pull[s] its signed package from the IP (the 'only egress', R-05.1)"*. Declared model: EP pulls. Implemented model: IP pushes. That is **D-007 again**, in the load-bearing direction, and it is why the IP knows the EP's address at all. Decide push-versus-pull and the address-holding cluster collapses on its own; leave it undecided and every reference has a local justification while the whole contradicts R-5.1.

**D-007 instance (vi) — a characterisation standing in for a trace, in the reasoning rather than the code.** 2026-08-04: the canonical composition was measured as *13 domain bindings and 13 `unwrap(dN.execute)` in each of two files, with no delegation between them* — a correct count — and reported as **"fully duplicated"**. That characterisation was accepted and a ruling issued on it: have the runner delegate to the direct composition. Tracing before building showed delegation would have collapsed thirteen domains into one stage and left eleven empty, breaking R-12.1 and C-12.6 — the invariants ADR-0061 §4 condition 2 exists to protect. The files share their *bindings and invocations*; their *arrangement* differs by design, and the arrangement is the constitutionally load-bearing part. **The count was evidence; "fully duplicated" was an untraced inference layered on top of it, and the ruling rested on the inference rather than the evidence.** Recorded because the class is not confined to the codebase: it applies to the reasoning about it, which is precisely what D-011 says the gates cannot verify. Resolved by extracting `canonical-domain-steps.ts` — bindings, `unwrapDomain` and the thirteen invocations shared; each composition keeps its own arrangement; the byte-identical equivalence test retained and now guarding exactly that remaining difference.

**D-011's wider limit — the governance suite verifies artefacts, not reasoning.** Three claims made during the 2026-08-04 session were load-bearing, marked INFERRED, and wrong. **(i) D-008's first cause** — the test count moving 310→313 was attributed to a stale incremental build, and a clean rebuild appeared to confirm it; the true cause was a concurrent edit already on disk, so the "confirming" rebuild could only ever return 313. **(ii) `verifyResponseSignature` / `isTransient`** — inferred to fall with the emitted transport binding; tracing found them to be declared ports of `src/runtime/execution-plane-transport.ts` with four conformance tests and a registered fault proof behind them. **(iii) `authoring-bridge.mjs`** — classified as a retirement target; ADR-0061 §6 step 6 says *re-point*, and that single misclassification manufactured a false finding that the canonical runtime could not seal an ExecutionPackage.

**None of the three was caught by a gate, and none could have been.** All 69 gates passed throughout. Each was caught by a different non-gate mechanism: (i) by stamping a measurement with the tree state it was taken against, (ii) by an explicit instruction to trace rather than infer, (iii) by re-reading the source document before standing behind a conclusion drawn from it. The suite verifies **artefacts** — files present, contracts matched, criteria satisfied, faults detected. It does not and cannot verify the **reasoning** that selects which artefacts to compare, and a correct measurement over a false premise passes every check while being worse than no measurement, because it carries the authority of having been measured. The controls that work here are procedural rather than executable: state the premise explicitly, trace what a conclusion rests on, stamp what a number was measured against, and keep a withdrawn claim visible so the shape of the error survives its correction.

**D-003 was found by measurement, not review.** The resilience suite asked whether replay protection survived a restart; the first version of that scenario reported a pass, because the replayed request *was* refused — as `bad-signature`, for an unrelated reason, caused by a second defect. Asserting on the **reason** rather than the status is what exposed both.

## 1a. Debt raised and closed

| # | Item | Found by | Closed by |
|---|---|---|---|
| **D-001** | Seven decisions closed in architecture documents before ADRs became mandatory | Manual review at M1.4 | ADR-0003...ADR-0008 |
| **D-004** | Token signing key generated per process, so a restart rejected every token the previous instance issued | Resilience scenario R-2 at M2.8 | Key loaded from state, using the custody model the certificate authority already had |
| **D-002** | Seven further decisions closed in M1.5 with no ADR — **the identical pattern, recurring immediately after D-001 was cleared** | `verify-adr-completeness.js`, first run | ADR-0009...ADR-0014 |
| **D-003** | AD-008 and AD-009 open while the degradation matrix already specified behaviour depending on them | Certification inventory | ADR-0015 |
| **D-004** | Rule 12 enforced in the Intelligence Plane only; the Execution Plane README cited the unversioned bootstrap file as an authority | `verify-ai-vendor-neutrality.js` | Execution Plane commit `f0432c8` — gate added under that repository's own history |

**D-002 is the most instructive entry.** The failure mode recurred within a single phase, under an explicit instruction to prevent it, and was caught by a machine rather than by discipline. That is the entire argument for building governance before runtime.

**D-004 is the second most instructive.** A rule enforced in one of two repositories is enforced in neither — and the fix could not be applied from the Intelligence Plane, because a change spanning both planes in one step is itself the sovereignty violation the boundary exists to prevent. Two repositories, two histories, two commits.


## 1a. Debt raised and closed during P1

| # | Item | Found by | Closed by |
|---|---|---|---|
| **D-001** | Seven decisions closed in architecture documents before ADRs became mandatory | Manual review at M1.4 | ADR-0003...ADR-0008 |
| **D-002** | Seven further decisions closed in M1.5 with no ADR - **the identical pattern, recurring immediately after D-001 was cleared** | `verify-adr-completeness.js`, first run | ADR-0009...ADR-0014 |
| **D-003** | AD-008 and AD-009 open while the degradation matrix already specified behaviour depending on them | Certification inventory | ADR-0015 |

**D-002 is the most instructive entry in this register.** The failure mode recurred within a single phase, under an explicit instruction to prevent it, and was caught by a machine rather than by discipline. That is the entire argument for building governance before runtime.

## 2. The policy

Debt is **fixed on discovery, not scheduled**. This register exists to prove it is empty — not to accumulate a backlog of deferred work.

After every milestone, scan for:

| Scan | Fix |
|---|---|
| Architecture drift | Immediately |
| Duplicate code | Immediately |
| Security issues | Immediately |
| Configuration drift | Immediately |
| Governance gaps | Immediately |
| Performance bottlenecks | Immediately |
| Maintainability issues | Immediately |

If an item genuinely cannot be fixed within the milestone, it is recorded here **with an owner and a resolving milestone**, and it blocks the milestone's exit criteria. An entry without a resolving milestone is not permitted.

## 3. Debt classes this programme structurally prevents

These are not aspirations. Each is prevented by a mechanism scheduled **before the code that could introduce it exists**. Each row is evidenced by a specific failure in the predecessor platform.

| Class | How it manifested | Preventing mechanism | Scheduled |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Declared-but-unbuilt** | A retention limit was customer-visible, schema-validated, API-served and console-rendered — and read by no code. Six of eight limit fields had no reader. Schema validation validated the *shape*, manufacturing the appearance of a live control. | Declaration and enforcement are one atomic change (D-012). Preferred form: derive the config schema from registered readers, making an unenforced field unrepresentable. Fallback: a build gate failing on any declared field with no reader. | M3.3 |
| **Duplicate governed term** | Two implementations of one hashing term produced divergent digests; evidence written by one and verified by the other reported tampering on untampered records. | A governed-terms registry with exactly one owning module each; CI fails on a second definition. Domain separation mandatory; algorithm version on every record from the first write. (D-007) | M2.2 |
| **Gates not running where work happens** | Gates triggered on `main`/`develop` while work happened on feature branches. One of five workflows produced signal where development occurred; a fitness test failed for an extended period unnoticed. | All gates on all branches; `NOT RUN` reported and treated as `FAIL`; branch protection defined as code inside the repository. (D-016) | M3.4 |
| **Silent capability failure** | A penetration engine was tier-listed as enabled and API-exposed with no runner on disk; its dispatch wrapper logged the miss and returned a soft failure. | Fail loudly by default (D-013); tier definitions validated against a capability registry at config load; every capability exercised end-to-end in CI from declaration (D-017). | M3.3, M7.3 |
| **The conformant path behind a flag** | The correct orchestration path was fully built and working but opt-in, so deployments ran the non-conformant default. Recurred on three further flags. | Defaults are architecture (D-011). A flag may protect a rollback path from the conformant default, never the reverse. | M1.1 (rule), M3.2 (gate) |
| **Unenforced retention** | Immutability was used to justify infinite retention; an append-only evidence store had no purge path. | A store cannot register without a retention source, a purge implementation, and a test proving data is unreadable after the limit. | M2.4, M6.3 |
| **Artefacts reaching no image** | Shared code sat above both repository roots and reached neither container image — invisible until containerisation, after 26 import sites accumulated. | Shared code is a versioned package from commit one (D-014); boundary check in strict mode from day one; image-closure check walking lazy requires; both images built in CI on every commit (D-015). | M3.2, M10.1 |
| **Conformance by accident** | The control plane conformed to the two-deployables rule because nothing called the function that would have broken it — not because anything prevented it. The dangerous function was exported. | Prefer structural impossibility to tests (D-018). Do not export what must never be called. | M1.1, M3.2 |
| **Environment-conditional controls** | A raw credential crossed the trust boundary outside production; startup validation shipped disabled by image default and therefore ran in no container. | Controls behave identically in every environment (D-019); no image default may weaken a control. | M3.2 |
| **Unlabelled degraded results** | Degradation was a log line rather than a structural field. | Degraded state is a required field; a degraded result is a distinct type the certification interface refuses (D-022). | M2.1 |
| **Single-mechanism enforcement** | The one rule enforced three independent ways never drifted; everything enforced once, or by prose, did. | Every constitutional rule enforced by at least three independent mechanisms (D-020). | M1.1, M3.2 |
| **Untested gates** | A control never observed to fail is indistinguishable from one that cannot fail. | Every gate ships with a recorded fault-injection proof (D-008). | M3.1 onward |

## 4. Standing rule

**A control that is declared but not executed is treated as absent, not as a gap.**

This is the single most important lesson inherited from the legacy platform. Its consequence: a governance record that lists controls which do not run is worse than no record, because it inverts the purpose of governance — it manufactures confidence rather than measuring it.
